## ER-660

From: Gregory, Thomas
To: Advocacy WDWG; Radu, Peter; Manager, C
Cc: Josiah Mulitauopele
Subject: Re: K. Panapanich, ADA request, as of 2-24-26
Date: Monday, March 09, 2026 12:54:06 PM
Hi Maud,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Kittivut Patanapanich.
The determinations below, including alternatives where feasible, were made by me in consultation with the
City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and staff.
Re your request for “temporary exemption from the towing program,” exempting Mr. Patanapanich’s vehicle
from the enforcement of parking rules would result in a fundamental alteration of the City’s parking
enforcement program. The City’s enforcement of local and State provisions governing parking is designed to
promote public safety, ensure access to limited on-street parking, facilitate street maintenance, and mitigate
neighborhood impacts associated with long-term vehicle storage. Exempting Mr. Patanapanich’s vehicle from
towing would undermine and fundamentally alter the purpose and application of these enforcement
provisions and interfere with the City’s ability to manage community impacts associated with unlawfully
parked vehicles.
Re your request for “Temporary stay on enforcement actions,” to the extent that Mr. Patanapanich is lodging
within the geographically designated abatement zone, individuals remain subject to relocation based on
health and safety conditions. Designating a specific exemption area for Mr. Patanapanich within the
abatement zone, or allowing him to remain in his current location for an indefinite amount of time, would
constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program. The City must address
conditions at this encampment site, including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public
health concerns. Allowing indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s ability to mitigate those risks
and would present a direct threat to public health and safety.
The City must address conditions at this encampment site, including those associated with ongoing
leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing continued indefinite occupation would interfere with
the City’s ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety. As an
alternative, the City will grant Mr. Patanapanich an accommodation to remain through March 10,
2026. The City cannot grant additional time without causing the fundamental alteration I have explained
above.
Regarding your request for “A visual aid,” visual signage throughout the City already makes clear where
vehicles (including RVs) may park and for how long they may park there. The City does not maintain a map of
parking signage throughout the City and could not do so, as parking signage often changes on a weekly basis.
Creating such a map would present an undue administrative burden for these reasons.
The City is sympathetic to Mr. Patanapanich circumstances and hopes he understands that under Title II of the
ADA, a public entity may deny a requested accommodation when it would result in a fundamental alteration of
its programs or impose an undue administrative burden, or where it would create a direct threat to health or
safety. Those considerations are what guide the determinations above.
This constitutes the City’s final determination regarding the accommodation requests submitted on Mr.
Patanapanich’s behalf. If Mr. Patanapanich disagrees with this determination, he may choose to file a
grievance through the City’s ADA grievance:
https://berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ADA%20Grievance%20Procedure%20and%20Form%20-
%20updated%20Feb2024%20-%20WCAG-compliant.pdf.

## ER-664

Good evening,
I am seeking to open an interactive disability accommodations process on behalf of Kittivut Patanapanich, in
accordance with City of Berkeley’s obligations under the Americans with Disabilities Act.
Full name: Kittivut Patanapanich
He does not have a phone or email address, so any updates can be sent to the email advocacy@wdwg.org and I
(Maud Kiewietdejonge) will pass on information as expediently as I can. 
Location of dwelling: Across from the Whole Foods loading dock, approximately 1250 Ninth Street, Berkeley
Type of dwelling: F150 Ford Truck, 1999 with CA license plate 6A51096
Disability accommodation for: Encampment abatement of Eighth and Harrison Streets and surrounding 1/3rd mile 
Mr. Patanapanich seeks accommodation for the following disability: Nerve damage sustained from a prolonged
illness, as a result of a bacterial infection originating in a cut on his right foot. The wound was aggravated and the
infection spread due to lack of consistent access to hygiene supplies and clean running water.
He was not able to be seen at a hospital, which resulted in the infection being prolonged for six months. This resulted
in significant nerve damage to his right foot, arm, and hand. These appendages have lost a significant amount of
strength, making it very difficult to lift heavy objects. He also experiences poor temperature regulation in his right
arm. 
Other aftereffects of this period include intense bouts of fatigue, which can last for two to three days, and bathroom
urgency. The former makes it difficult to move around and make arrangements to move his vehicle out of the sweep
zone, and the latter leaves him dealing with the task of finding a hygienic place to use the restroom with his level of
fatigue, which can create a burden on his time. 
He cannot perform a tight grip with his right hand, which is further exacerbated by the cold and physical exertion.
Intermittent numbness in the affected foot, arm, and hand comes on suddenly and makes it risky to drive. This means
he needs community support to operate the vehicle as needed for him to be in compliance with the city’s
requirements.
The nerve damage in his hand results in significant burning pain and makes the area more susceptible to severe pain
from relatively minor injuries, exertion, and cold outside temperatures, which have prevented him from doing the
tasks needed to get his registration up to date.
The bosses at Whole Foods and the construction site for the slated new Berkeley fireman’s headquarters respectively
both gave him hygiene supplies to help care for the wound. He has their names on file and can, with their consent,
provide their contacts. Either of these individuals can confirm their knowledge of his injury if needed. A neighbor
called Lifelong Medical on his behalf upon learning of the infection, but he was not able to be seen. 
Lifelong Medical 837 Addison St, Berkeley CA 94710
(510)-981-4100 would be his easiest point of access to a care provider. 
We respectfully request the following disability accommodations:
— We ask the city to use their discretion to grant Mr. Panapanich temporary exemption from the towing
program due to the additional time needed to get his registration up to date, and make arrangements to move his
vehicle in compliance with the zone specified on the coming public notices regarding the Harrison corridor, when
they are posted. —Temporary stay on enforcement actions at his location —A visual aid in the form of a legible map or diagram, showing a list of viable, legally designating permissible
parking spots covering 12x25 feet. His restricted movement due to the pain in his foot prevents him from seeking and
confirming the viability of new areas without input from the city.
While the abatement notices in the surrounding area cover the location of his dwelling, Mr. Patanapanich did not
himself receive a paper notice notifying him of the city’s relocation requirements. We understand that the area is to be
newly noticed soon, and so are seeking solutions that accommodate him before that happens.
We ask that a response regarding the interactive process be given by the close of business of this work week, or prior
to the posting of the new notices, whichever is sooner. 
Thank you,

## ER-666

From: Gregory, Thomas
To: Berkeley Homeless Union
Cc: Radu, Peter; Vance-Dozier, Okeya
Subject: Re: REQUEST: ADA Accommodation for Quinton Cocklereese
Date: Monday, March 09, 2026 2:50:38 PM
Hi Yesica,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Quinton Cocklereese.
You have submitted substantially similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals over the past several
months, and you have previously been advised that such requests would constitute a fundamental alteration
of the City’s operations, impose an undue burden, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form of
reasonable accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities. Under Title II
of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or undue burden, because of the
impact that a particular request would have on the City and its programs or services. As I have previously
informed you, those impacts to the City are the same, regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for
their request. As such, even if there were a nexus between Mr. Cocklereese’s requests and his required
relocation, the following determinations would still apply.
The determinations below, including alternatives where feasible, were made by me in consultation with the
City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and staff.
Re your first enumerated request (“Executive Functioning Assistance”), the City does not provide such a
service, and Title II of the ADA does not require that the City create a new service. Center for Independent
Living, in Berkeley, provides no-cost peer support to folks with executive functioning deficits.
https://thecil.org/
Re your second enumerated request (“Designated Point of Contact”), Mr. Cocklereese may contact me at
(510) 981-6418 and/or at ada@cityofberkeley.info if he has questions.
Re your third enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice with Step-by-Step Instructions”), the City is
unable to grant a request for 30 days’ notice to vacate the encampment. Providing that extended notice would
constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program because it would prevent
the City from effectively addressing public health hazards, including the presence of Leptospira identified at
the encampment.
The requested 30-day notice would also fundamentally alter the City’s ability to implement its encampment
management program in a way that flexibly responds to health and safety conditions in the community. The
presence of Leptospira at the encampment illustrates the types of public health risks that require the City to
act promptly to protect residents, staff, and the broader community. For these reasons, the City cannot
accommodate the requested extended notice period.
Re your fourth enumerated request (“Low-Stimulation Meetings”), the City is not requiring Mr. Cocklereese
to attend any meetings. Whenever Mr. Cocklereese arranges a meeting with a City staff person, he is welcome
to contact me to explain the specific requests he has to minimize the possibility that he will become
overstimulated, and I will facilitate those accommodation requests as best as possible for each meeting,
consistent with my duties as the City’s ADA Coordinator.
Re your fifth enumerated request (“Safe Relocation Option”), no sanctioned campsite exists in Berkeley at
this time. The City’s encampment regulations generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square feet on
sidewalks and prohibit lodging or storage of property in certain other locations, including medians and City
building curtilage. Individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine square feet,
provided pedestrian access is maintained and the area is not designated as a no-lodging zone. Even then, all
individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and safety conditions.
Following posted signage and applicable codes and regulations when relocating will reduce the likelihood of
enforcement in a subsequent location, although enforcement decisions remain condition dependent. If Mr.
Cocklereese is seeking assistance or services, he may contact HRT or visit https://berkeleyca.gov/safetyhealth/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may be available to him.
Re your sixth enumerated request (“Non-Congregate Shelter Placement”), shelter placement is not itself a
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 8 of 165
ER-666

## ER-667

reasonable accommodation under Title II of the ADA. The provision of shelter is a public service that may be
available depending on eligibility, capacity, and prioritization criteria. The ADA requires reasonable
modifications to policies to ensure equal access to an existing program; it does not require the creation of new
housing resources or the guarantee of shelter.
Once Mr. Cocklereese is offered shelter through the City’s Homeless Response Team (HRT), he may request
reasonable modifications related to the shelter environment itself, and those would be evaluated in that
context. Mr. Cocklereese is encouraged to contact HRT to learn about currently available non-congregate
shelter opportunities. He may also contact 211 for information regarding regional resources.
Re your seventh enumerated request (“Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing”),
individuals remain subject to relocation based on health and safety conditions. Designating a specific
exemption area for Mr. Cocklereese within the abatement zone, or allowing him to remain in his current
location for an indefinite amount of time until housing is secured, would constitute a fundamental alteration of
the City’s encampment management program. The City must address conditions at this encampment site,
including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing
indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct
threat to public health and safety.
Further, designating a specific exemption area for Mr. Cocklereese for an indefinite amount of time until
housing is secured would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management
program. The City must address conditions at this encampment site, including those associated with ongoing
leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing continued indefinite occupation would interfere with the
City’s ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety. As an
alternative, the City will grant Mr. Cocklereese an accommodation to remain through March 10, 2026. The
City cannot grant additional time without causing the fundamental alteration I have explained above.
Re your eighth enumerated request (“Recognition of Dog as Medical Equipment”), dogs are not
equipment, medical or otherwise. To the extent that the animal(s) in question are service animals then they
may go/be anywhere that Mr. Cocklereese may go/be. To the extent that the animal(s) in question are
emotional support animals then they may accompany Mr. Cocklereese to the extent that he is actively
participating in any City-sponsored shelter program provided that Mr. Cocklereese provides documentation
from a qualified professional confirming that the animal(s) in question are necessary for Mr. Cocklereese’s
mental wellbeing.
This constitutes the City’s final determination regarding the accommodation requests submitted on Mr.
Cocklereese’s behalf, and this determination is based upon the reasons described above, and due to the fact
that the request are not supported by sufficient information showing the disability-related need for the
requested accommodation, and because the requested accommodation would impede actions necessary to
protect public health. If Mr. Cocklereese disagrees with this determination, he may choose to file a grievance
through the City’s ADA grievance process:
https://berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ADA%20Grievance%20Procedure%20and%20Form%20-
%20updated%20Feb2024%20-%20WCAG-compliant.pdf. Best,

## ER-668

WARNING: This is not a City of Berkeley email. Do not click links or attachments unless you trust the sender and know the content is safe.
Hi Mr. Gregory and Homeless Response Team,
I am writing on behalf of Quinton Cocklereese, a disabled member of the Berkeley Homeless Union
currently residing on Harrison Street encampment, between 7th and 6th streets, to formally submit a
request for reasonable accommodations.
Please find below the information required to initiate this request:
1. Disability Verification
Mr. Cocklereese lives with documented disabilities, including:
Severe anxiety
Depression
Bipolar disorder 
Borderline Personality Disorder (BPD)
Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD)
Oppositional Defiant Disorder (ODD)
Other related mental health impairments affecting his ability to navigate public spaces and interact 
with officials
These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including concentrating, interacting
with others, regulating emotions, and handling stress. His fear of public visibility makes it difficult for him to
engage with City staff, access services, or comply with enforcement directives without direct support.
Mr. Cocklereese has authorized the release of information regarding his disability. We are in the process of
obtaining medical verification from his provider and will forward it to you as soon as it becomes available.
Given the urgency of the enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process
based on the disabilities self-reported in his signed accommodation request form (attached), pending
receipt of formal verification.
2. Specific Circumstances Prompting This Request
In your previous response to a similar accommodation request, you asked for clarification regarding what
"specific circumstances or actions" prompted the request. The circumstances are now clear and urgent:
The City posted a Public Notice dated February 17, 2026, ordering all persons encamped in the Harrison
Street area to vacate. This imminent enforcement action creates the specific circumstances requiring
accommodation.

## ER-669

3. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the February 17, 2026 Public Notice
—will, if applied to Mr. Cocklereese without modification, foreseeably aggravate his disabilities in the
following ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
His anxiety and fear of public visibility require predictability and support. Sudden displacement forces him
into public view and interaction, triggering acute anxiety, panic, and an inability to function or comply with
City orders.
b) Requirement to engage with officials independently
The enforcement process requires individuals to interact with City staff, pack belongings, and coordinate
logistics—all of which require public visibility and engagement. For Mr. Cocklereese, these demands are
paralyzing due to his disability.
c) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Mr. Cocklereese for disability-related inability to comply—such as being unable to engage with
officials or manage logistics independently—would constitute discrimination under the ADA.
d) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of his belongings—including his tent and items essential to daily survival—would be devastating.
For someone with his disabilities, losing familiar, essential items can trigger acute psychological crises and
further cognitive decline.
e) Forced relocation without an accessible destination
Moving Mr. Cocklereese from one outdoor location to another does not achieve the City's stated goal of
"moving all people indoors." It merely destabilizes him, disrupts any connection to services, and
exacerbates his cognitive impairments.
4. Requested Accommodations
Based on Mr. Cocklereese's completed accommodation request form and his disabilities, we respectfully
request the following accommodations:
1) Executive Functioning Assistance
Mr. Cocklereese requires direct support to help with tasks such as:
Scheduling and coordinating services
Understanding and tracking deadlines
Communicating with City staff on his behalf
Packing and organizing his belongings
Navigating the relocation process step by step
This assistance must be provided by a designated support person (which could include a Union
representative, peer support worker, or City staff assigned to this role) who can serve as a consistent,

## ER-670

trusted liaison.
2) Designated Point of Contact
A single, consistent point of contact from HRT or the ADA Coordinator to help Mr. Cocklereese navigate all
aspects of the relocation process. Frequent changes in staff or messengers will cause confusion, increase
anxiety, and render him unable to comply.
3) Advance Written Notice with Step-by-Step Instructions
Due to his anxiety and difficulty with public engagement, Mr. Cocklereese requires advance written notice
of any enforcement, cleaning, or relocation activity affecting his location—with clear, step-by-step
instructions and at least 30 days' advance notice—to allow him time to process the information and prepare
with his support person's assistance.
4) Low-Stimulation Meetings
Any meetings regarding his accommodations or relocation must be conducted in a low-stimulation
environment with:
Advance scheduling
A quiet, private space
His designated support person present
Limited number of unfamiliar officials
Breaks as needed
Clear written follow-up
5) Safe Relocation Option
Mr. Cocklereese requires a clearly designated, safe location where he can relocate with his support
person's assistance, without fear of citation, arrest, or property destruction.
6) Non-Congregate Shelter Placement
If shelter placement is part of his relocation, Mr. Cocklereese requires a private, low-stimulation setting
(hotel room) where he can manage his mental health disabilities without the overwhelming demands of
congregate living.
7) Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing
If no non-congregate shelter is immediately available, Mr. Cocklereeset must be permitted to remain
temporarily in the 8th & Harrison area until an accessible room becomes available. Stability is medically

assist with his mental health disabilities. The dog must be treated as medical equipment, not personal
property, and must remain with him at all times.

From: Gregory, Thomas
To: Berkeley Homeless Union

Subject: Re: REQUEST: Disability Accommodation for Brenda Bolares
Date: Monday, March 09, 2026 4:53:58 PM
Hi Yesica,
You have submitted substantially similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals
over the past several months, and you have previously been advised that such requests
would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s operations, impose an undue
burden, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form of reasonable
accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities.
Under Title II of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or
undue burden, because of the impact that a particular request would have on the City
and its programs or services. As I have previously informed you, those impacts to the
City are the same, regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for their request.
The determination below was made by me in consultation with the City Manager’s Office
and relevant City departments and staff.
The City has reviewed your recent request for reasonable accommodations on behalf of
Ms. Bolares. The City is highly concerned that the email misrepresents significant facts
regarding Ms. Bolares’ circumstances and the accommodations being requested.
According to City records and recent staff observations, Ms. Bolares does not reside at
the Harrison encampment. Because your request appears to misrepresent these facts,
and the City cannot rely on the representations made in your correspondence, the City
will not grant the requested accommodations.
In any case, I am also confirming that the City did not ever receive any documentation
from any qualified professional regarding Ms. Bolares.
Best,
Thomas Gregory / ADA Program Coordinator
City of Berkeley, Public Works Department
1947 Center Street, 5th Floor
Berkeley, CA 94704

I am writing on behalf of Brenda Bolares, a disabled member of the Berkeley
Homeless Union currently residing in the 8th and Harrison encampment, to follow-up
with her request for reasonable accommodations.
As you are aware, Ms. Bolares's accommodation request has been ongoing since
February 2025. The interactive process was never completed. We are reviewing this
request now because the City has issued a new Public Notice of Encampment
Closure dated March 2, 2026, ordering all persons in this area to vacate by March 9. 
The interactive process must be completed before enforcement occurs, and
accommodations must be in place to prevent disability-related harm.
Please find below the information required to continue this interactive process:
1. Disability Verification
Ms. Bolares lives with documented learning and cognitive disabilities that
substantially limit one or more major life activities, including:
Problem-solving
Decision-making
Executive functioning issues
Adaptation to frequent change
Completing daily life tasks independently
Maintaining stability in outdoor environments

## ER-674

These conditions mean that relocation, displacement, and sudden changes create
cognitive overload, making it nearly impossible for her to perform basic survival tasks
or comply with complex instructions.
Ms. Bolares receives care from the LifeLong Medical Street Team. Given the urgency
of the enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process
based on the disabilities self-reported in her prior requests, pending receipt of formal
verification.
2. History of Interactive Process (2025)
Ms. Bolares first requested accommodations in February 2025 in response to a
previous abatement order in the encampment. Key points from that process include:
February 3, 2025: BHU submitted an initial request noting Ms. Bolares's 
cognitive disabilities and requesting permission to remain in place, exemption 
from relocation, protection of her tent, and exemption from citation/arrest.
February 4, 2025: You denied her original requests, stating they would 
"fundamentally alter the nature of the City's nuisance-abatement efforts." You 
invited new accommodation requests.
February 5, 2025: BHU proposed alternative accommodations including a 
phased relocation plan, relocation assistance, and a designated stability zone. 
You did not provide a substantive response to these proposals.
April 15, 2025: BHU followed up, reiterating the alternative proposals and 
requesting confirmation on whether the City could assist with a non-congregate 
hotel placement through LifeLong.
May 2, 2025: You responded, accepting her disabilities but requesting 
professional verification of the nexus between her disabilities and the requested 
accommodations. You directed her to HRT for shelter information.
The interactive process stalled at that point, and Ms. Bolares remains at the
encampment with the same disabilities and accommodation needs.
3. Specific Circumstances Prompting This Renewed Request

## ER-675

The City has now posted a Public Notice dated March 2, 2026, requiring all persons
in the Harrison Street area to vacate. This imminent enforcement action creates the
specific, urgent circumstances requiring accommodation. Without the
accommodations previously requested—and still needed—Ms. Bolares cannot
comply with this order without exacerbating her disabilities.
4. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the March 2,
2026 Public Notice—will, if applied to Ms. Bolares without modification, foreseeably
aggravate her disabilities in the following ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
Her cognitive disability requires predictability, stability, and adequate time to process
information. Sudden displacement triggers cognitive overload, confusion, and an
inability to function or make decisions, leaving her unable to plan or comply with City
orders.
b) Physical and cognitive demands of relocation
The relocation process requires problem-solving, decision-making, and adaptation to
new environments—all of which are directly impaired by her disability. She cannot
independently pack, organize, or transport her belongings, nor can she navigate
unfamiliar locations without support.
c) Loss of her tent
Her tent provides a structured, familiar environment that helps her manage her
disability-related challenges. Removing it eliminates her only stable, disabilityadapted shelter, directly impacting her ability to perform daily life tasks.
d) 9-square-foot limit on sidewalks
Compliance is impossible without abandoning her tent and essential belongings. Her
cognitive disability prevents her from making rapid decisions about what to keep or
discard, and the stress of the situation would overwhelm her ability to act.
e) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Ms. Bolares for disability-related inability to comply—such as being unable
to understand instructions, make decisions under pressure, or navigate unfamiliar
systems—would constitute discrimination under the ADA. The stress of such
encounters would deepen her cognitive overload and trauma.
f) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of her belongings—including items essential to daily survival—would be
devastating. For someone with cognitive disabilities, losing familiar, essential items

5. Requested Accommodations
Based on Ms. Bolares's prior requests, the history of this case, and her ongoing

1) Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing
If no non-congregate shelter or safe relocation option is immediately available, Ms.
Bolares must be permitted to remain temporarily at her current location until an
alternative is secured. Stability is medically necessary to prevent cognitive overload
and loss of function.
2) A Safe Relocation Option
Ms. Bolares requires a clearly designated, safe location where she can relocate
without fear of citation, arrest, or property destruction. This location must:
Be accessible to someone with cognitive disabilities

Be within reasonable distance to services
Allow her to rest without immediate threat of further displacement
3) Non-Congregate Shelter Placement
If shelter placement is part of her relocation, Ms. Bolares requires a private, lowstimulation setting (hotel room) where she can manage her cognitive disabilities
without the overwhelming demands of congregate living. LifeLong Medical Street
Team has been working with her to access such placement—we request City
assistance in facilitating this.
4) Phased Relocation Plan
Ms. Bolares requires a phased relocation plan with:
Extended timelines for compliance (at least 30 days)
Clear, step-by-step instructions broken into manageable tasks

overload, making it nearly impossible for her to perform basic survival tasks.

4) Exemption from citation and arrest related to the abatement order – Because her disability prevents her from complying with relocation orders
and her dwelling was not documented in the city’s abatement report as
imposing a health and safety concern, enforcing penalties against her would

Under Title II of the ADA (28 C.F.R. § 35.130(b)(7)), public entities must
reasonably modify policies, practices, or procedures to accommodate
individuals with disabilities unless the city can demonstrate that the
modification would fundamentally alter the nature of the program or
impose an undue burden. City’s Violation if Denied:
Frequent displacement disrupts her ability to function, worsening her

environment. Removing her tent eliminates her only stable and disability-adapted 
shelter, which directly impacts her ability to manage daily life tasks.
Issuing citations or arresting her for failing to relocate would

Subject: Re: REQUEST: Disability Accommodation for Jermaine Lee White

Hi Yesica,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Jermaine Lee White.
You have submitted substantially similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals over the past several
months, and you have previously been advised that such requests would constitute a fundamental alteration
of the City’s operations, impose an undue burden, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form of
reasonable accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities. Under Title II
of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or undue burden, because of the
impact that a particular request would have on the City and its programs or services. As I have previously
informed you, those impacts to the City are the same, regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for
their request. As such, even if there were a nexus between Mr. White’s requests and his required relocation,
the following determinations would still apply.
The determinations below, including alternatives where feasible, were made by me in consultation with the
City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and staff.
Re your first enumerated request (“City-Allocated Lot Camping Space”), no sanctioned campsite exists in
Berkeley at this time. The City’s encampment regulations generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square
feet on sidewalks and prohibit lodging or storage of property in certain other locations, including medians and
City building curtilage. Individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine square
feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained and the area is not designated as a no-lodging zone. Even then,
all individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and safety conditions.
Following posted signage and applicable codes and regulations when relocating will reduce the likelihood of
enforcement in a subsequent location, although enforcement decisions remain condition dependent. If
Mr. White is seeking assistance or services, he may contact HRT or visit https://berkeleyca.gov/safetyhealth/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may be available to him.
Re your second enumerated request (“Temporary Stay-in-Place at 8th & Harrison”), individuals remain
subject to relocation based on health and safety conditions. Designating a specific exemption area for Mr.
White within the abatement zone, or allowing him to remain in his current location for an indefinite or extended
amount of time, would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program.
The City must address conditions at this encampment site, including those associated with ongoing
leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s
ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety.
Designating a specific exemption area for Mr. White for an indefinite or extended amount of time would
constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program. The City must address
conditions at this encampment site, including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public
health concerns. Allowing continued indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s ability to mitigate
those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety. As an alternative, the City will grant
Mr. White an accommodation to remain through March 10, 2026. The City cannot grant additional time
without causing the fundamental alteration I have explained above.
Re your third enumerated request (“Non-Destruction of Belongings and Shelter”), the City is unable to
accommodate a request to store belongings. Public Works staff cannot sort, bag, or handle these items, even
with personal protective equipment, because the City’s Public Health Officer has determined that, based on
recently observed conditions at the encampment, the belongings are highly likely to be contaminated with
Leptospira through rat urine, rat carcasses, live rats, or mud. Handling such items would pose a significant
public health risk to City staff, constituting an undue administrative burden. In addition, the City cannot store
potentially contaminated items, as the Public Health Officer has advised that Leptospira can survive on
contaminated materials for weeks. Requiring the City to accept such items into storage would impose an
undue burden, given the substantial administrative effort that would be needed either to mitigate those risks or
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 31 of 165
ER-689
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (54 of 289)

## ER-690

to decontaminate the storage facility after the items were kept there. This is consistent with the City’s storage
policy that generally excludes the storage of items presenting a health or safety risk. Because the City cannot
store abandoned property likely to be contaminated with Leptospira, the City has no option other than to
dispose of such property, which is the functional equivalent of destroying such property. The City has
repeatedly conducted direct public health outreach at the encampment and provided several rounds of
notices informing residents of its inability to handle or store belongings, and it has repeatedly communicated
the same information to you, to try to minimize negative impacts on those remaining at the encampment.
Re your fourth enumerated request (“Physical Assistance with Transporting Belongings”), the City is
unable to satisfy this request as doing so would be unduly burdensome for the same reasons described in the
preceding paragraph.
Re your fifth enumerated request (“Peer Support”), the City does not provide such a service, and Title II of
the ADA does not require that the City create a new service. Center for Independent Living, in Berkeley,
provides no-cost peer support to people with disabilities who desire such support. https://thecil.org/
Re your sixth enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice with Step-by-Step Instructions”), the City is
unable to grant a request for 30 days’ notice to vacate the encampment. Providing that extended notice would
constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program because it would prevent
the City from effectively addressing public health hazards, including the presence of Leptospira identified at
the encampment.
The requested 30-day notice would also fundamentally alter the City’s ability to implement its encampment
management program in a way that flexibly responds to health and safety conditions in the community. The
presence of Leptospira at the encampment illustrates the types of public health risks that require the City to
act promptly to protect residents, staff, and the broader community. For these reasons, the City cannot
accommodate the requested extended notice period.
The steps that Mr. White takes to comply with the abatement order, as well as the sequence of such steps, are
up to Mr. White. I recommend: (1) gather all possessions not being abandoned, then (2) remove self and
unabandoned possessions from the posted abatement zone.
Re your seventh enumerated request (“Assistance Connecting to Mental Health Services”), Mr. White/BHU is welcome to schedule a mutually convenient time for me to meet Mr. White in person
(anywhere within Berkeley) so that I can accompany him as he uses his phone to call Berkeley Mental Health to
schedule an intake appointment with Berkeley Mental Health.
Re your eighth enumerated request (“Non-Congregate Shelter Placement”), shelter placement is not itself
a reasonable accommodation under Title II of the ADA. The provision of shelter is a public service that may be
available depending on eligibility, capacity, and prioritization criteria. The ADA requires reasonable
modifications to policies to ensure equal access to an existing program; it does not require the creation of new
housing resources or the guarantee of shelter.
Once Mr. White is offered shelter through the City’s Homeless Response Team (HRT), he may request
reasonable modifications related to the shelter environment itself, and those would be evaluated in that
context. Mr. White is encouraged to contact HRT to learn about currently available non-congregate shelter
opportunities. He may also contact 211 for information regarding regional resources.
Re your ninth enumerated request (“ADA-Exempted Footprint”), the request for an indefinite or permanent
footprint on any public sidewalk, even provided a four-foot pedestrian clearance is maintained, cannot be
granted. Public sidewalks serve multiple legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel (and
associated accessibility compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial activity conducted under
active City permits, and placement of utilities and public infrastructure. Sidewalk widths vary considerably,
and in many locations a four-foot clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once curb ramps,
turning radii, and other obstructions are considered. Granting Mr. White an exemption from the nine-squarefoot limitation would fundamentally alter the City’s management of the public right-of-way and conflict with
other legally protected uses. For those reasons, this request cannot be approved.
Re your tenth enumerated request (“Recognition of Dog as Medical Equipment”), dogs are not equipment,
medical or otherwise. To the extent that the animal(s) in question are service animals then they may go/be
anywhere that Mr. White may go/be. To the extent that the animal(s) in question are emotional support
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 32 of 165

## ER-691

animals then they may accompany Mr. White to the extent that he is actively participating in any Citysponsored shelter program provided that Mr. White provides documentation from a qualified professional
confirming that the animal(s) in question are necessary for Mr. White’s mental wellbeing.
This constitutes the City’s final determination regarding the accommodation requests submitted on
Mr. White’s behalf, and this determination is based upon the reasons described above, and due to the fact that
the request are not supported by sufficient information showing the disability-related need for the requested
accommodation, and because the requested accommodation would impede actions necessary to protect
public health.
If Mr. White disagrees with this determination, he may choose to file a grievance through the City’s ADA
grievance process:
https://berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ADA%20Grievance%20Procedure%20and%20Form%20-

Hi Mr. Gregory and Homeless Response Team,
I am writing on behalf of Jermaine Lee White, a disabled member of the Berkeley Homeless Union
currently residing in a tent/structure at the west corner of 8th and Harrison Streets, to follow up on his
request for reasonable accommodations.
As you are aware, Mr. White's accommodation request has been ongoing since January 2025. We are
reviving this request now because the City has issued a new Public Notice of Encampment Closure dated
February 17, 2026, ordering all persons in this area to vacate. 
The interactive process must be completed before enforcement occurs, and accommodations must be in
place to prevent disability-related harm. Please find below the information required to continue his request:
1. Disability Verification
Mr. White lives with documented disabilities, including:
Anxiety and other mental health complications that make completing tasks difficult
Executive functioning impairments affecting his ability to organize, initiate, and follow through on 
tasks
These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including concentrating, task initiation,

ER-691
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (56 of 289)

## ER-692

organization, decision-making, regulating emotions, and handling stress. His executive dysfunction means
he cannot independently structure and complete the complex tasks required for relocation without direct
support.
Mr. White receives care from the Lifelong Medical Street Team. We are in the process of obtaining medical
verification from his provider and will forward it to you as soon as it becomes available. Given the urgency
of the enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process based on the
disabilities self-reported in his signed accommodation request form (attached), pending receipt of formal
verification.
2. History of Interactive Process (2025)
Mr. White first requested accommodations in January 2025 in response to a previous abatement order. Key
points from that process include:
January 17, 2025: BHU submitted an initial request noting Mr. White's need for physical assistance, 
clear instructions, and mental health support.
January 21, 2025: You responded that physical assistance would be available during enforcement, 
offered to explain the Fact Sheet, and provided a referral to Berkeley Mental Health.
February 3, 2025: BHU clarified that Mr. White's need for physical assistance stems from executive 
dysfunction, not a physical disability, and requested pre-abatement support, step-by-step guidance, 
and assistance navigating mental health services.
February 3, 2025: You responded that physical assistance would only be available in connection with 
the storage service, declined to commit to ongoing instructions, and reiterated the BMH referral.
February 4, 2025: BHU proposed alternative accommodations. You did not provide a substantive 
response to these proposals.
The interactive process was never completed, and Mr. White remains at the encampment with the same
disabilities and accommodation needs.
3. Specific Circumstances Prompting This Renewed Request
The City has now posted a Public Notice dated February 17, 2026, requiring all persons in the Harrison
Street area to vacate. This imminent enforcement action creates the specific, urgent circumstances
requiring accommodation. Without the accommodations previously requested—and still needed—Mr. White
cannot comply with this order without exacerbating his disabilities.
4. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the February 17, 2026 Public Notice
—will, if applied to Mr. White without modification, foreseeably aggravate his disabilities in the following
ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
His anxiety and executive dysfunction require predictability and adequate time to process information.
Sudden displacement triggers panic, task paralysis and emotional dysregulation, leaving him unable to plan
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 34 of 165
ER-692
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (57 of 289)

## ER-693

or comply with City orders.
b) Physical demands of relocation
Due to his executive dysfunction, Mr. White cannot independently organize, pack, or transport his
belongings. The physical tasks of relocation are impossible without direct assistance because he cannot
structure the work or follow through without support.
c) 9-square-foot limit on sidewalks
Compliance is impossible without abandoning his tent (his medical equipment and only accessible shelter)
and essential belongings. His executive dysfunction prevents him from making rapid decisions about what
to keep or discard, and the space is too small for him to rest or store his necessities.
d) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Mr. White for disability-related inability to comply—such as being unable to organize his
belongings, pack within time limits, or make decisions under pressure—would constitute discrimination
under the ADA. The stress of such encounters would deepen his anxiety and render him even less able to
comply.
e) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of his belongings—including items essential to daily survival—would be devastating. For
someone with anxiety and executive dysfunction, losing familiar, essential items can trigger acute
psychological crises.
5. Requested Accommodations
Based on Mr. White's completed accommodation request form, the history of this case, and his ongoing
disabilities, we respectfully request the following accommodations:
1) City-Allocated Lot Camping Space
Mr. White requests a designated, City-allocated lot where he can camp safely with a footprint larger than 9
square feet for his dwelling. This would provide the stability and predictability his anxiety and executive
dysfunction require while he awaits permanent housing.
2) Temporary Stay-in-Place at 8th & Harrison
If no City lot or non-congregate shelter is immediately available, Mr. White must be permitted to remain
temporarily at the 8th & Harrison location until a safe relocation option is secured. Stability is medically
necessary to prevent aggravation of his anxiety and executive dysfunction.
3) Non-Destruction of Belongings and Shelter
We request a formal assurance that Mr. White's belongings and shelter will not be destroyed during any
enforcement action. His tent is his medical equipment and only home, and losing it would cause irreparable
harm.
4) Physical Assistance with Transporting Belongings
Due to his executive dysfunction, Mr. White requires City staff assistance with:
Organizing and packing his belongings
Lifting and transporting items to any new location
Navigating the storage process if applicable

Provide consistent, trusted support
Help him understand and track deadlines
Break tasks into manageable steps
Offer reminders and check-ins
Advocate on his behalf when needed
6) Advance Written Notice with Step-by-Step Instructions
Due to his executive dysfunction, Mr. White requires advance written notice of any enforcement, cleaning,

7) Assistance Connecting to Mental Health Services

Scheduling an intake appointment
Following through with ongoing care

If Mr. White must remain outdoors temporarily, we request an accommodation allowing a tent footprint

He is not blocking pedestrian access, meaning a clear path remains for others to pass safely; and

6. Request for Interactive Process Meeting
We respectfully request an in-person meeting with the ADA Coordinator, HRT, Mr. White, a Union

Low-stimulation, held in a quiet, private space
Scheduled with advance notice
Accompanied by clear written follow-up afterward
7. Immediate Next Steps Requested
To fulfill the ADA's requirements and avoid foreseeable harm to Mr. White, we ask the City to:
Pause any enforcement activity toward Mr. White pending full review of this request and completion 
of the interactive process;
Confirm receipt of this request within 48 hours;
Provide available dates for a low-stimulation meeting within the next week;
Provide a substantive response to each of the requested accommodations listed above;
Suggest alternatives if any of the requested accommodations are deemed not feasible, so that we 
can continue the interactive process in good faith.
We ask that the City not delay the interactive process while awaiting medical documentation, given the
imminent enforcement deadline.
Mr. White has been waiting for accommodations since January 2025. He is ready and willing to engage in
good faith. We look forward to finally completing this interactive process and ensuring his disability-related
needs are met.

On Wed, Feb 5, 2025 at 10:42 AM Berkeley Homeless Union <berkeley.homeless.union@gmail.com> wrote:
Mr. Gregory,
Thank you for your response and for offering clarification on available assistance. I appreciate your
willingness to engage in this discussion, and I want to ensure that we are fully exploring reasonable
accommodations under the ADA’s interactive process.
To clarify, Mr. White’s need for physical assistance is directly related to his executive functioning
challenges, which make it difficult for him to structure and complete tasks independently. Without
assistance, he cannot effectively organize his belongings or prepare for relocation, which creates a
significant barrier to compliance with the abatement order.
In the interest of identifying workable solutions, we would like to propose the following alternatives for
accommodations:
Pre-Abatement Support – While physical assistance will be available during enforcement, we request
that City staff also provide scheduled assistance before the abatement deadline to help Mr. White
organize his belongings and navigate the storage process.
Step-by-Step Compliance Plan – Instead of a one-time explanation, Mr. White would benefit from a
structured, step-by-step breakdown of what he needs to do, with periodic check-ins to ensure he stays on
track. Assigning a liaison to provide reminders or check-ins before the deadline would help mitigate his
executive functioning challenges.
Regular Clean-Ups – Since Public Works staff previously provided clean-up services at the
encampment, we request that these services ensure that Mr. White has access to necessary support in
maintaining his space clean before the abatement date. If this is not possible, we ask the City to
acknowledge how the lapse in services has contributed to current site conditions.
Mental Health Navigation Assistance – While we appreciate the referral to BMH and phone number,
Mr. White may struggle with navigating the system independently. We request City staff assist him in
making initial contact with mental health services to ensure he can access the support he needs.
We hope that these suggestions will help bridge the gap between the City's enforcement policies and Mr.
White’s disability-related challenges. If a meeting would be helpful to refine these accommodations,
we remain available to coordinate a time.
Please let us know by the end of the day, whether these accommodations can be implemented or if the
City has alternative proposals.
Thank you for your time and consideration.

Subject: Re: REQUEST: Disability Accommodation for Jermaine Lee White
Dear Mr. Gregory,
Thank you for your response. I appreciate your willingness to provide physical assistance as needed, but I
want to clarify the nature of Mr. White’s disability-related needs and ensure that his request is fully understood
under the ADA’s interactive process.
1) Clarification of Mr. White’s Need for Physical Assistance
You asked for elaboration on why Mr. White requires physical assistance in organizing and cleaning his
campsite due to his mental health conditions.
Mr. White experiences anxiety and other mental health challenges that impair his executive
functioning.
Executive dysfunction is a well-documented effect of anxiety and other mental health conditions.
It affects task initiation, organization, and follow-through, making it difficult for Mr. White to structure 
and complete tasks independently.
His need for physical assistance is not due to a physical disability alone but rather an 
accommodation for his executive functioning limitations.
The City has recognized executive function challenges as a legitimate basis for accommodations in
past cases.
If the City provides accommodations for people with cognitive disabilities that impair their ability 
to complete required tasks, then it must apply the same standard to Mr. White.
 Request: Ensure City staff do not just assist with compliance on the day of enforcement but also
help with organization and cleaning in the days leading up to February 10 to allow him to prepare
properly.
2)The City’s Failure to Provide Regular Clean-Ups Contributed to Current Conditions
Mr. White noted that Public Works staff stopped coming for weekly clean-ups in November, leading to
the accumulation of items. The City cannot cite “unsanitary conditions” without acknowledging its own
role in failing to maintain regular clean-ups.
 Request: The City should resume scheduled clean-ups immediately so that Mr. White can receive the
assistance he needs before the deadline. (City staff were not present this weekend due to the rain storms).
If clean-ups do not resume, the City must acknowledge that its failure to provide services contributed to
the current state of the encampment.
3) Individualized Instructions Must Be Part of the Interactive Process
You offered to read and explain the Fact Sheet attached to the abatement notice. While this is helpful, it
does not fully address Mr. White’s need for clear and supportive instructions tailored to his

4) Mental Health Support Should Be Accessible Without Barriers

On Tue, Jan 21, 2025 at 10:15 AM Gregory, Thomas <TGregory@berkeleyca.gov> wrote:
Hi Yesica,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Jermaine White.
Physical assistance is certainly available as needed, but I am not understanding your statement that
"Due to his mental health conditions, Mr. White requires...physical assistance to help him organize and
clean...." Please elaborate on his disability-related need for physical assistance.
If/when an enforcement action occurs, staff will be available on-site to assist with compliance with the
abatement order. Such staff will be able to help with any physical tasks consistent with the order. 
If/when an enforcement action occurs, staff will also be available to help clean any areas that require
cleaning. Although the City encourages all campers to maintain their living environment in as cleanly a
condition as possible, the City does not expect anyone to clean anything they are unable to clean due to
a disability.
Please note that even if Mr. White cleans the area where he is currently living, his belongings will need to
be relocated by 2/10. Therefore, if Mr. White would like to avail himself of the City's offer to store
belongings, please do...the sooner, the better. The contact info re this service is on the abatement
notice/order.
Regarding the request for "clear and supportive instructions," would it help if I were to meet with him to
do my best to read/explain the Facts Sheet that was attached to the abatement notice? If so, I can do
that. I assume that BHU understands the requirements of the abatement notice, and I would invite you
to join me in trying to make that info understood by a BHU member.
Regarding the request for access to a therapist or mental health support, the City has an adult services
clinic that provides no-cost mental health support, including access to licensed clinicians and
therapists, for individuals who have Medi-Cal and meet other eligibility requirements. The staff who
heads up that unit is named Allyson Nakayama, who can be reached at 510-981-5244 and/or
anakayama@berkeleyca.gov. Please note that only general information about BMH services are able to
provided via electronic communication.

Dear Mr. Gregory and Homeless Response Team,
I am writing on behalf of Jermaine Lee White, a resident of the encampment at 8th and Harrison and a union
member. Mr. White experiences anxiety and other mental health challenges that make completing tasks and
managing daily responsibilities particularly difficult for him.
We are requesting a disability accommodation for Mr. White in response to the City’s order to abate the
public nuisance at the encampment. His campsite was significantly impacted by the severe winter rainstorms,
leaving it in need of reorganization and cleanup. Mr. White also noted that Public Works staff stopped coming
for weekly clean-ups in November, which led to the accumulation of more items. Due to his mental health
conditions, Mr. White requires:
1. Physical assistance to help him organize and clean his campsite.
2. Clear and supportive instructions to guide him in meeting the requirements outlined in the abatement
notice.
3. Access to a therapist or mental health support, which would help him stay focused on tasks while also
providing an environment where he feels heard and understood during this process.
These accommodations would help Mr. White comply with the abatement order while addressing the
unique challenges posed by his mental health needs. Please find his request attached to this email and let us
know if any additional information or documentation is required to process this disability request.
Thank you for your attention and support on this matter! Your assistance would make a significant difference in
ensuring his ability to comply with the City’s requirements.
Best,
Yesica Prado
Berkeley Homeless Union Representative

## ER-702

From: Gregory, Thomas To: Advocacy WDWG
Cc: mcbear5678@gmail.com; Josiah Mulitauopele; Radu, Peter; Manager, C
Subject: Re: Urgent Disability accommodation for Melissa Cheatwood Date: Monday, March 09, 2026 2:55:34 PM
Hi Maud,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Ms. Melissa Cheatwood. The determinations below, including
alternatives where feasible, were made by me in consultation with the City Manager’s Office and relevant
City departments and staff.
Regarding your request for “A minimum of 90 days extension, individuals residing in the designated
abatement zone remain subject to relocation based on health and safety conditions. Ms. Cheatwood has
had notice of the need to relocate since at least February 19, 2026, which is now more than two weeks
ago. Under the circumstances, the City must clear the area to effectively address the ongoing
leptospirosis public health concern. Rats can and often do hide in and around vehicles near Harrison,
which significantly complicates and undermines the City’s ability to safely abate the outbreak if vehicles
remain in place for extended periods.
As such, designating a specific exemption area for Ms. Cheatwood within the abatement zone or allowing
her or her vehicle to remain in the current location for an extended period of time, would constitute a
fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program. The City must address
conditions at this site, including those associated with the ongoing leptospirosis-related public health
concerns. Allowing a 90-day extension would also interfere with the City’s ability to mitigate those risks
and would present a direct threat to public health and safety.
Because of these conditions, the City cannot provide a 90-day notice period. However, as an alternative
accommodation, the City will allow Ms. Cheatwood to remain through March 10, 2026. The City cannot
grant additional time without creating the fundamental alteration or direct threat described above.
Regarding your request for “Recognition of van as medical equipment, reasonable accommodations
under the ADA involve modifications to a public entity’s policies, practices, or procedures. The City does
not classify vehicles as any particular type of equipment, and therefore there is no City policy, practice, or
procedure applicable to this request that could be modified through the reasonable accommodation
process. Vehicle classifications are established by state and federal law, including regulatory frameworks
under the California Vehicle Code, not by municipal designation. Because the City does not determine
how vehicles are classified under those legal frameworks, it cannot issue a formal acknowledgment that
Ms. Cheatwood’s van constitutes medical equipment. Additionally, the request does not identify a nexus
between Ms. Cheatwood’s disability and any modification to a City policy, program, or procedure.
Further, exempting Ms. Cheatwood’s vehicle from the enforcement of parking rules would result in a
fundamental alteration of the City’s parking enforcement program. The City’s enforcement of local and
state provisions governing parking is designed to promote public safety, ensure access to limited onstreet parking, facilitate street maintenance, and mitigate neighborhood impacts associated with longterm vehicle storage. Exempting Ms. Cheatwood’s vehicle from towing would undermine the purpose and
application of these enforcement provisions and interfere with the City’s ability to manage community
impacts associated with unlawfully parked vehicles.
Ms. Cheatwood’s vehicle may be towed if it remains in a designated “no parking” area or if it is out of
compliance with California registration requirements, including if registration has been expired for more
than six months, as authorized under California Vehicle Code section 22651(o). The City cannot grant an
exemption from these requirements. To the extent the request relates to the cost of bringing the vehicle
into compliance, that concern is financial rather than disability-related, and therefore does not qualify as
a reasonable accommodation under the ADA. The California Vehicle Code permits law enforcement to
tow vehicles with registration expired for more than six months, and the City enforces these requirements
consistently to ensure public safety and compliance with state law. The City also strives to exercise
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 44 of 165
ER-702
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (67 of 289)

## ER-703

flexibility with unhoused community members where possible, which is reflected in the fact that some
individuals have been permitted to keep unregistered vehicles parked in the City for extended periods
when those vehicles did not create community impacts.
Regarding your request for “the City to designate a specific place to park,” the City does not provide
designated parking spaces to residents or visitors. Parking availability is governed by posted signage
throughout the City, which identifies where vehicles may park and for how long. The City does not
maintain a comprehensive map of parking signage, and creating and maintaining such a map would
present an undue administrative burden, particularly because parking conditions and restrictions change
frequently.
The City is sympathetic to Ms. Cheatwood’s circumstances and hopes she understands that under Title II
of the ADA, a public entity may deny a requested accommodation when it would result in a fundamental
alteration of its programs or impose an undue administrative burden, or where it would create a direct
threat to health or safety. Those considerations are what guide the determinations above.
To the extent restroom access is a concern, the City maintains public restrooms throughout the City, and
you may be able to assist Ms. Cheatwood in identifying nearby public facilities or other places of public
accommodation, such as other grocery stores, where restrooms are available to the public.
In addition, the City contracts with Dorothy Day House, which operates a drop-in center that provides
services including meals, showers, laundry, free clothing, lockers, mail services, internet access, respite,
housing support, DMV and ID assistance, workforce training, and medical and addiction referrals to
partner social service agencies. These services may be helpful resources for Ms. Cheatwood:
https://www.dorothydayhouse.org/services
This message constitutes the City’s final determination regarding the accommodation requests
submitted on Ms. Cheatwood’s behalf, and this determination is based upon the reasons described
above, and due to the fact that the requests are not supported by sufficient information showing the
disability-related need for the requested accommodation, and because the requested accommodation
would impede actions necessary to protect public health. If Ms. Cheatwood disagrees with this
determination, she may choose to file a grievance through the City’s ADA grievance procedure:
https://berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ADA%20Grievance%20Procedure%20and%20Form%20-
%20updated%20Feb2024%20-%20WCAG-compliant.pdf. Best,
Thomas Gregory / ADA Program Coordinator

## ER-708

Disability accommodation for: Encampment abatement of Eighth and Harrison Streets
I am seeking to open an interactive accommodations process on behalf of Ms. Melissa Cheatwood, as required of the
City of Berkeley by the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA). Ms. Cheatwood received a notice on the 19th saying she
is required to move her van, her only dwelling, from Harrison Street by the 24th. This gives her less than a week to
relocate. 
Melissa suffers from multiple documented chronic illnesses that severely impact her immune system function, and
therefore overall mobility, strength, and daily needs. She lives with and must manage the following conditions: 
Rheumatoid arthritis 
Celiac disease/IBD 
Co-occurring severe food allergies, which can result in anaphylaxis. 
Chronic gastritis and stomach ulcers 
History of reproductive tumors, with one currently active 
The combination of these conditions pose significant limitations to her mobility.
Her conditions hinder her in walking, standing for long periods, and lifting heavy objects. She is chronically anemic
because of her digestive disorders. This causes severe fatigue, results in periods of extreme weight loss and attendant
muscle wasting, from which it takes six months minimum to recover. This makes operating a vehicle and making viable
arrangements to move impossible within the given timeframe. 
As a result of her celiac disease and other illnesses, she deals with bathroom urgency on a regular basis and must have
immediate easy access to a private, sanitary bathroom at all times, which she currently finds at the nearby Whole
Foods. 
Her vehicle is a van, not an RV, so it doesn’t have a built-in bathroom. She must be able to relocate somewhere where
she can meet this basic need. Immediate bathroom access allows her to maintain proper hygiene, which is essential to
to maintain her health and keep her job.
Her maintenance of these medical conditions is time-intensive, as she must manage the refills of multiple
medications, and go through longer periods of rest, both to avoid aggravating symptoms and to manage active flareups. She requires help moving from friends/support people, at a time when chronic pain is not too prohibitive. 
Congregate shelters are medically contraindicated. The variability of other shelter inhabitants’ health and heightened
risk for communicable diseases mean her vehicle is her only option for a safe dwelling. Removing it would deprive her
of her only shelter, and of life-saving medical care. The van is the only safe place for her to store all of the medications
needed to manage her multiple chronic illnesses. All of her medications and other necessary supplies are delivered to
Whole Foods. Proximity to a place where her medications get delivered is non-negotiable. She also must have a nearby
point of access to food which can accommodate the medically necessary diet modifications for celiac.
Ms. Cheatwood needs immediate easy access to public transport. She works in San Francisco, for which there are a
more limited number of transit stops than intra-East Bay. Being within a very short walking distance of a stop is crucial
for her to keep her job, which supports thousands of dollars worth of monthly medical costs. Public transit access is
also essential because she often must go to the emergency room for needs related to her illnesses, and the amount of
ambulance rides would put her in severe medical debt. 
Her work hours are variable, and she frequently has to return to her dwelling late at night. Her current location is the
place that she feels safest doing so. Areas directly outside of the posted hazard zone are not sufficiently lit as to be
safe, nor do they have public transit stops which she could access without having to exert herself so much that it
aggravates her disabilities.
We require confirmation of whether the City is engaging with the interactive process, and will urgently need specific
confirmation of whether the following accommodations will be granted, or, if applicable, which items need to be
further discussed, by Monday, February 23rd, at 10am.

## ER-709

The accommodations needed are as follows:
A minimum of 90 days extension, to make arrangements to move her vehicle to a safe location where her medical
needs can be met. 
Recognition of van as medical equipment, which cannot be destroyed, seized, and discarded during any
enforcement action. This clean, private place to rest and store medication is indispensable.
For the City to designate a specific place to park, which is more than 12x21 feet, and for which there would be an
exemption from time-limited parking enforcement. Moving her vehicle every 72 hours would severely aggravate her
disabilities. This designation can take the form of a simple and legible visual map, diagram, or a written list, showing or
specifying where she may legally relocate within the city. Possession of a visual aid will allow her to communicate the
accommodations to city enforcement entities as necessary.
She has been treated by Lifelong Medical, 837 Addison St, Berkeley CA 94710
(510)-981-4100. 
However, she has received her most recent and most frequent medical care at Highland Hospital. She has paperwork
from her most recent ER visit to substantiate her conditions, and Olivia deBree, Nurse Practitioner with Lifelong
Medical, can verify her medical conditions if needed. She can provide other medical records if afforded sufficient time
to go through the request process. 
Ms. Cheatwood is ready and willing to engage with the interactive process and we look forward to working
collaboratively to ensure her disability-related needs are met.

Subject: Re: REQUEST: ADA Accommodation for Alice Barbee
Hi Yesica,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Ms. Alice Barbee. I personally spoke with Ms. Barbee at the
encampment on January 23, 2026, and again on February 19, 2026. On January 23 I asked her, “do you have
any disabilities that would prevent you from complying with an order to vacate this area within a week?” and
she replied, “No, not necessarily.” On February 19 I asked Ms. Barbee, “Do you have any disabilities that
would prevent you from vacating this campsite by February 24th?” and on that date she made clear that she
was disabled and that, while her disability would not hinder her ability to vacate herself from the abatement
zone, her disability would hinder her ability to vacate her property from the abatement zone. That request,
along with these new additional requests submitted by you, are addressed below.
You have submitted substantially similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals over the past several
months, and you have previously been advised that such requests would constitute a fundamental alteration
of the City’s operations, impose an undue burden, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form of
reasonable accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities. Under Title II
of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or undue burden, because of the
impact that a particular request would have on the City and its programs or services. As I have previously
informed you, those impacts to the City are the same, regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for
their request. As such, even if there were a nexus between Ms. Barbee’s new requests and her required
relocation, the following determinations would still apply. These determinations, including alternatives where
feasible, were made by me in consultation with the City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and
staff.
Re your first enumerated request (“Permanent Housing Placement”), shelter, permanent or otherwise, is
not a form of reasonable accommodation but, rather, a service that the City provides when shelter
opportunities are available. If Ms. Barbee is seeking assistance or services, she may contact HRT or visit
https://berkeleyca.gov/safety-health/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may be
available to her. If Ms. Barbee is offered an available shelter placement and that particular placement does
not meet her access and functional needs, Ms. Barbee can request ADA modifications at that
point. Additionally, the City does not operate nor control permanent supportive housing opportunities, but
HRT can assist Ms. Barbee with updating her records in the HMIS, if she would like to engage with them.
