Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (75 of 289)

## ER-711

Re your second enumerated request (“Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing”), to the
extent the request seeks an indefinite pause on enforcement at the current location, the City cannot grant that
request. Allowing an individual to remain in place notwithstanding the need to abate conditions associated
with confirmed leptospirosis would require the City to suspend or override the need for a timely abatement to
control vectors and prevent the spread of communicable disease, which is a fundamental part of its
encampment management and public health response programs. Such a modification would fundamentally
alter the nature of those programs, which are specifically designed to enable prompt intervention when
encampments pose imminent risks to public health and safety.
An open-ended suspension of enforcement in these circumstances would also prevent the City from carrying
out required vector control measures and would undermine its ability to respond consistently to emergent
health hazards across the public right-of-way. The City’s planned actions are consistent with City Council
Resolution No. 71,513-N.S., which authorizes enforcement and abatement where encampments present
imminent health hazards, even when shelter is not immediately available. As an alternative, the City will
grant Ms. Barbee an accommodation to remain through March 10, 2026. The City cannot grant additional
time without causing the fundamental alteration I have explained above.
Re your third enumerated request (“Non-Congregate Shelter Placement”), shelter is not a form of
reasonable accommodation but, rather, a service that the City provides when shelter opportunities are
available. If Ms. Barbee is seeking assistance or services, she may contact HRT or visit
https://berkeleyca.gov/safety-health/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may be
available to her. If Ms. Barbee is offered an available shelter placement and that particular placement does
not meet her access and functional needs, Ms. Barbee can request ADA modifications at that point.
Re your fourth enumerated request (“A Safe Location to Relocate”), no sanctioned campsite exists in
Berkeley at this time. The City’s encampment regulations generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square
feet on sidewalks and prohibit lodging or storage of property in certain other locations, including medians and
City building curtilage. Individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine square
feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained and the area is not designated as a no-lodging zone. Even then,
all individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and safety conditions.
Following posted signage and applicable codes and regulations when relocating will reduce the likelihood of
enforcement in a subsequent location, although enforcement decisions remain condition dependent. If Ms.
Barbee is seeking assistance or services, she may contact HRT or visit https://berkeleyca.gov/safetyhealth/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may be available to her.
Re your fifth enumerated request (“Physical Assistance with Relocation”), under normal circumstances,
City staff could help a disabled member of an encampment physically pack and transport safe and hygienic
possessions if the individual is moving those possessions to a City-sponsored shelter. However, staff
currently cannot sort, bag, or handle such items, even with personal protective equipment, because the City’s
Public Health Officer has determined that, based on recently observed conditions at the encampment, the
belongings are highly likely to be contaminated with Leptospira through rat urine, rat carcasses, live rats, or
mud. Handling such items would pose a significant public health risk to City staff, constituting an undue
administrative burden.
Re your sixth enumerated request (“Storage for Essential Belongings”), the City cannot store potentially
contaminated items, as the Public Health Officer has advised that Leptospira can survive on contaminated
materials for weeks. Requiring the City to accept such items into storage would impose an undue burden,
given the substantial administrative effort that would be needed either to mitigate those risks or to
decontaminate the storage facility after the items were kept there. This is consistent with the City’s storage
policy that generally excludes the storage of items presenting a health or safety risk. The City has repeatedly
conducted direct public health outreach at the encampment and provided several rounds of notices informing
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 53 of 165
ER-711
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (76 of 289)

## ER-712

residents of its inability to handle or store belongings, and it has repeatedly communicated the same
information to you, to try to minimize negative impacts on those remaining at the encampment.
Re your seventh enumerated request (“ADA-Exempted Footprint”), the request for an indefinite or
permanent footprint on any public sidewalk, even provided a four-foot pedestrian clearance is maintained,
cannot be granted. Public sidewalks serve multiple legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel
(and associated accessibility compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial activity conducted
under active City permits, and placement of utilities and public infrastructure. Sidewalk widths vary
considerably, and in many locations a four-foot clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once
curb ramps, turning radii, and other obstructions are considered. Granting Ms. Barbee an exemption from the
nine-square-foot limitation would fundamentally alter the City’s management of the public right-of-way and
conflict with other legally protected uses. For those reasons, this request cannot be approved.
Re your eighth enumerated request (“Accommodation for Emotional Support Animals”), as I have
previously informed you, emotional support animals may accompany Ms. Barbee to the extent that she is
actively participating in any City-sponsored shelter program provided that Ms. Barbee provides
documentation from a qualified professional confirming that the animal(s) in question are necessary for Ms.
Barbee’s mental wellbeing.
Re your ninth enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement”), ”), the City is unable
to grant a request for 30 days’ additional notice to vacate the encampment. Providing that additional notice
would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program because it would
prevent the City from effectively addressing public health hazards, including the presence of Leptospira
identified at the encampment. As part of its ongoing encampment management program, the City has
repeatedly provided both written and verbal notices regarding the need to relocate since January 2025.
Additionally, I personally spoke with Ms. Barbee on January 23, 2026, and February 19, 2026, to provide verbal
notice. As such, Ms. Barbee has been provided with over 40 days’ notice, and this accommodation has
already been met.
Re your tenth enumerated request (“Visual Aid / Map of Permissible Locations”), the ADA requires public
entities to modify policies and procedures when necessary to ensure that individuals with disabilities have
equal access to public programs. However, the City is not obligated to create individualized maps or preapproved locations that guarantee immunity from enforcement. As previously explained, the City’s
encampment-related requirements are established through a combination of municipal code provisions,
administrative regulations, and City Council resolutions, all of which are subject to change based on public
health conditions, safety concerns, and legal requirements. Park rules prohibit overnight camping and
unattended property exceeding ten square feet without a permit. The Berkeley Municipal Code and
Administrative Regulation 10.2 generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square feet on sidewalks and also
prohibit lodging or storage of property on medians and within City building curtilage.
In limited circumstances, individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine
square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained, facilities are not obstructed, and the location is not
designated as a no lodging area. Even in those circumstances, all individuals remain subject to enforcement
based on health and safety impacts, nuisance conditions, and compliance with applicable law. Because
enforcement determinations are location specific, condition dependent, and responsive to changing
circumstances, it is not possible for the City to identify locations where camping would be categorically free
from enforcement.
Further, no map identifying enforcement-free locations exists in City records. Creating a map that would
illustrate areas less likely to draw enforcement would require extensive staff time, site-specific analysis, and
physical measurement of sidewalks and public spaces throughout the City and would therefore constitute an
undue administrative burden under Title II. As an alternative, the City has summarized the applicable

## ER-713

regulatory framework to assist in understanding how enforcement decisions are made but cannot provide
individualized guarantees regarding future enforcement outcomes.
Re your eleventh enumerated request (“Temporary Sanitation Services”), the City has, in limited
circumstances, provided portable restrooms, trash receptacles, and limited waste services at certain
encampments as population-level public health measures. Decisions about whether to deploy these services
depend on site conditions, public health guidance, operational feasibility, and broader encampment
priorities, including the allocation of limited resources. These services are deployed as part of the City’s
encampment management program and are not provided on an individual basis. Also, when an encampment
is cleared, associated sanitation and waste services are discontinued.
Providing sanitation services to an individual who remains outside following an encampment closure would
require the City to fundamentally alter how its encampment management program operates. Under current
practice, sanitation resources, such as portable restrooms, trash receptacles, and waste services, are
deployed at certain active encampments as population-level public health measures based on the number of
people present, site conditions, and available resources. These services are designed to address conditions at
encampment sites, not to operate as individual services.
Extending those services to a single individual after an encampment has been cleared would shift the program
from a site-based public health measure to an individualized service. Doing so would require the City to
fundamentally alter the program and redirect limited sanitation resources in a way that the program is not
structured to support. For that reason, granting the request would fundamentally alter how the City’s
encampment management program functions.
In addition, providing on-demand individualized sanitation services would effectively create a new program or
service. Title II of the ADA requires reasonable modifications to existing programs but does not require public
entities to create entirely new programs.
The City does, however, contract with community partners that provide sanitation and related services to
individuals in need. For example, Dorothy Day House operates a drop-in center at its main shelter in
downtown Berkeley that is open seven days a week, year-round. The center offers meals, showers, laundry,
clothing, lockers, mail services, internet access, respite space, housing support, ID and DMV assistance,
workforce training, and referrals for medical and addiction services. More information about these services is
available here: https://www.dorothydayhouse.org/services. The City also maintains public restroom facilities
at various locations throughout the City that are available for public use.
Re your twelfth enumerated request (“Pause on Enforcement”), to the extent the request seeks an
indefinite pause on enforcement at the current location, the City cannot grant that request. Allowing an
individual to remain in place notwithstanding the need to abate conditions associated with confirmed
leptospirosis would require the City to suspend or override the need for a timely abatement to control vectors
and prevent the spread of communicable disease, which is a fundamental part of its encampment
management and public health response programs. Such a modification would fundamentally alter the nature
of those programs, which are specifically designed to enable prompt intervention when encampments pose
imminent risks to public health and safety.
An open-ended suspension of enforcement in these circumstances would also prevent the City from carrying
out required vector control measures and would undermine its ability to respond consistently to emergent
health hazards across the public right-of-way. The City’s planned actions are consistent with City Council
Resolution No. 71,513-N.S., which authorizes enforcement and abatement where encampments present
imminent health hazards, even when shelter is not immediately available

Hi Mr. Gregory and Homeless Response Team,
I am writing on behalf of Alice Barbee, a disabled member of the Berkeley Homeless Union currently
residing on the west side of 8th Street, along the U.S. Post office, to formally submit a request for
reasonable accommodations.
Please find below the information required to initiate this request:
1. Disability Verification
Ms. Barbee lives with documented, long-term disabilities, including:
Herniated disk (C6-C7) for over 20 years
Congestive Heart Failure for 5 years
Neuro damage
These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including walking, standing, sitting for
extended periods, lifting, carrying, sleeping, breathing and regulating physical stress. Due to the severity of
her conditions, sudden physical exertion or acute stress could trigger serious cardiac events and

## ER-715

exacerbate her neuro damage and chronic pain.
Ms. Barbee receives care from the Lifelong Street Medicine Team. We are in the process of obtaining
medical verification from her provider and will forward it to you as soon as it becomes available. Given the
urgency of the enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process based on
the disabilities self-reported in her signed accommodation request form (attached), pending receipt of
formal verification.
2. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the February 17, 2026 Public
Notice—will, if applied to Ms. Barbee without modification, foreseeably aggravate her disabilities in the
following ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
Her congestive heart failure and neuro damage require predictability and stability. Sudden displacement
triggers acute stress, which elevates heart rate and blood pressure, endangering her cardiac health. The
physical demands of rapid packing and moving could precipitate a medical emergency.
b) Physical demands of relocation
Due to her herniated disk, neuro damage and heart failure, Ms. Barbee cannot lift heavy objects, walk long
distances, stand for extended periods, or engage in sustained physical activity. Requiring her to pack and
move her belongings without assistance would cause severe pain, potential injury, and life-threatening
cardiac strain.
c) 9-square-foot limit on sidewalks
Compliance is physically impossible without abandoning her tent (her medical equipment and only
accessible shelter), emotional support animals and essential belongings. The space is too small for her
and dogs to rest, store her necessities, or protect her body from the elements—critical for managing her
heart condition and chronic pain.
d) Separation from emotional support animals
Ms. Barbee relies on her dogs as emotional support animals (ESAs) to manage the psychological toll of
her disabilities. These animals are not pets—they are medical equipment that provide emotional regulation
and a sense of safety. Separating her from her dogs would trigger acute psychological distress and
destabilize her mental health. Additionally, she requires a bigger space for her dogs, as they cannot be
confined to a small space without causing her additional stress.
e) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Ms. Barbee for disability-related inability to comply—such as moving too slowly, being unable to
pack within time limits, or being unable to fit her belongings into 9 square feet—would constitute
discrimination under the ADA. The stress of such encounters could trigger a cardiac event.
f) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of her belongings—including medications, medical supplies and items essential to daily survival
—would be harmful. For someone with congestive heart failure and neuro damage, losing access to
medications and adaptive items can lead to serious medical deterioration.
g) Forced relocation without an accessible destination
Moving Ms. Barbee from one outdoor location to another does not achieve the City's stated goal of
"moving all people indoors." It merely destabilizes her, disrupts access to medical care, and exposes her to
repeated physical strain and stress—each time increasing her risk of cardiac crisis.
h) Lack of sanitation services

## ER-716

Her conditions require access to clean water, restroom facilities, and basic sanitation. Without these, her
risk of infection and medical complications increases significantly.
3. Requested Accommodations
Based on Ms. Barbee's completed accommodation request form and her multiple disabilities, we
respectfully request the following accommodations:
1) Permanent Housing Placement (Small House or Apartment with Yard)
Ms. Barbee requires a small house or apartment with a small yard to accommodate herself and her
emotional support animals. This is not merely a preference—it is a medical necessity because:
Her dogs require outdoor space for their well-being, which directly impacts her emotional stability;
Her mobility limitations make navigating multi-story buildings difficult and dangerous;
2) Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing
If no permanent housing is immediately available, Ms. Barbee must be permitted to remain temporarily in
the 8th & Harrison area until a suitable unit becomes available. Stability is medically necessary to prevent
the physical and psychological stress that could trigger a cardiac event.
3) Non-Congregate Shelter Placement (with outdoor access)
As an interim measure while awaiting permanent housing, Ms. Barbee requires a hotel room or other
private, low-stimulation shelter unit with:
A private bathroom
Access to outdoor space (yard or designated dog area) for her emotional support animals
No strict curfews or rigid rules that trigger anxiety and stress
Congregate settings are medically inappropriate as they increase risk of respiratory infection and will not
allow all her animals to stay with her.
4) A Safe Location to Relocate
If shelter is unavailable, Ms. Barbee requires a clearly designated, safe location where she can relocate
without fear of citation, arrest, or property destruction. This location must:
Be accessible to someone with significant mobility limitations and heart failure
Provide access to basic sanitation (restroom, clean water, trash pickup)
Include space for her dogs to relieve themselves safely
Be within reasonable distance to food, social and medical services
5) Physical Assistance with Relocation
Due to her severe physical limitations, Ms. Barbee requires City staff assistance with lifting and
transporting her medical supplies and essential belongings to any new location. This assistance must be:
Scheduled in advance
Completed at a pace that does not cause physical strain or pain
Broken into manageable segments with rest breaks as needed
6) Storage for Essential Belongings (especially medical supplies)
We acknowledge the City's concern regarding leptospirosis. However, we request that the City identify a
safe storage option for Ms. Barbee's essential items—particularly her medications, medical supplies and
items necessary for daily survival. 
7) ADA-Exempted Footprint
A permanent accommodation allowing Ms. Barbee to maintain a tent footprint larger than the standard 9-

and her essential belongings—on any public sidewalk where:
She is not blocking pedestrian access, meaning a clear path remains for others to pass safely; and
If located on a narrower sidewalk, the opposite side of the street remains clear for alternative
pedestrian access.
This modification recognizes that for Ms. Barbee, her tent functions as medical equipment and her only
accessible shelter, helping prevent the aggravation of her life-threatening disabilities.
8) Accommodation for Emotional Support Animals (Dogs)
Ms. Barbee relies on her dogs as emotional support animals (ESAs) to manage the psychological impact
of her disabilities. These animals are medical equipment prescribed by her healthcare provider. We
request:
Formal recognition that her dogs are ESAs and must remain with her at all times;
Confirmation that any housing or shelter placement will permit her dogs without breed, weight, or fee
restrictions upon presentation of documentation;
That any placement include access to outdoor space (yard or designated dog area) for her dogs;
That dog food, supplies and bedding be treated as essential medical supplies and not discarded
during any enforcement;
That during any relocation, staff permit her to maintain control of her dogs at all times;
9) Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement
Due to the life-threatening nature of her conditions, Ms. Barbee requires advance written notice of any
enforcement, cleaning, or relocation activity affecting her location—with at least 30 days' advance notice—
to allow her time to:
Process the information without acute stress
Arrange for medical support during the transition
Prepare her belongings and dogs at a pace that does not endanger her health
Coordinate with her healthcare provider
10) Visual Aid / Map of Permissible Locations
A simple, visual map, diagram, list, or document showing where she may legally relocate within the city,
including areas where tents are permitted, sanitation services are available and dog relief areas exist. This
reduces uncertainty and stress.
11) Temporary Sanitation Services
If Ms. Barbee must remain outdoors temporarily, we request access to:
Portable restrooms within reasonable distance
Clean water for drinking and hygiene
Trash disposal services to maintain sanitary conditions
Designated areas for her dogs to relieve themselves
These are essential for managing her heart condition, preventing infection and maintaining her dogs'
health.
12) Pause on Enforcement
Ms. Barbee requires a complete pause on enforcement activities directed at her until an accessible,
permanent housing unit with outdoor access becomes available. Repeated displacement would cause
cumulative physical and psychological harm that could prove fatal given her congestive heart failure.

13) Low-Stimulation Meetings
Any meetings regarding her accommodations or relocation must be conducted in a low-stimulation

Advance scheduling
A quiet, private space
Permission to bring her support animals

A single, consistent point of contact
This accommodates her stress-sensitive cardiac condition and need for predictability.
14) Designated Point of Contact
A single, consistent point of contact from HRT or the ADA Coordinator to help Ms. Barbee navigate the

4. Request for Interactive Process Meeting
We respectfully request an in-person meeting with the ADA Coordinator, HRT, Ms. Barbee, a Union
representative, and her medical provider (Lifelong Street Medicine Team) to discuss these
accommodations in detail and ensure a thorough, individualized interactive process. This meeting must be
low-stimulation, held in a quiet, private space, and permit her support animals to accompany her.
5. Immediate Next Steps Requested
To fulfill the ADA's requirements and avoid foreseeable risk of death or serious harm to Ms. Barbee, we
ask the City to:
Pause any enforcement activity toward Ms. Barbee pending full review of this request and completion
of the interactive process;
Confirm receipt of this request within 48 hours;
Provide available dates for a low-stimulation meeting within the next week;
Provide a substantive response to each of the requested accommodations listed above;
Suggest alternatives if any of the requested accommodations are deemed not feasible, so that we
can continue the interactive process in good faith.
We ask that the City not delay the interactive process while awaiting medical documentation, given the
imminent enforcement deadline and the life-threatening nature of Ms. Barbee's conditions.
Ms. Barbee is ready and willing to engage in good faith. We look forward to working collaboratively to
ensure her disability-related needs are met and her life is protected.

Subject: Re: REQUEST: ADA Accommodation for Erik Alexander Johnson Coon
Hi Yesica, 
Thank you for your follow-up regarding Mr. Erik Alexander Johnson Coon. I have reviewed
this renewed request in light of the March 2, 2026 Notice of Encampment Closure and in
consultation with the City Manager and relevant departmental staff. I have also
considered the verification letter previously provided by Mr. Coon’s medical provider.
Based on the information already submitted and prior communications, I believe the
City has sufficient information to evaluate the requests. The City has not requested
additional information from a medical provider, as none is necessary to issue a
determination. Where feasible, alternatives are identified below.
At the outset, I acknowledge that the March 2, 2026 Notice of Encampment Closure
establishes that enforcement activity is imminent in the defined geographic area. The
City has therefore evaluated these requests in the context of a pending action. I have
assessed each requested accommodation and identified alternatives where feasible. 
1. Safe, Quiet Relocation Option (Non-Congregate Shelter or Designated Safe
Location)
Shelter placement is not itself a reasonable accommodation under Title II of the ADA.
The provision of shelter is a public service that may be available depending on eligibility,
capacity, and prioritization criteria. The ADA requires reasonable modifications to
policies to ensure equal access to an existing program; it does not require the creation
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 62 of 165
ER-720
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (85 of 289)

## ER-721

of new housing resources or the guarantee of a specific housing outcome.
Once Mr. Coon is offered non-congregate shelter through the City’s Homeless Response
Team (HRT), he may request reasonable modifications related to the shelter
environment itself, and those would be evaluated in that context.
No sanctioned campsite exists in Berkeley, and there are no enforcement-free zones.
The City’s encampment-related requirements are established through municipal code
provisions, administrative regulations, and City Council resolutions that apply citywide
and are subject to change based on public health, safety, and legal considerations.
Enforcement determinations are location-specific and condition-dependent.
As an alternative, Mr. Coon is encouraged to contact HRT to learn about currently
available shelter opportunities. He may also contact 211 or visit this link:
https://berkeleyca.gov/safety-health/homeless-services for information regarding
shelter and homeless resources. 
2. Modification of Enforcement Operations (Noise Triggers)
You request that sirens, high-decibel alarms, and other loud equipment not be used in
proximity to Mr. Coon during abatement operations. Compliance with the posted Notice,
by vacating the defined area by March 9, 2026, would eliminate proximity to any
equipment used during the abatement.
In addition, the City cannot agree to disable, suppress, or refrain from using required
safety equipment during abatement operations. Heavy machinery such as dump trucks
and loaders are legally required to utilize audible backup alarms and other warning
systems to protect workers and members of the public. These features are mandated by
safety standards and municipal risk management practices.
Modifying enforcement operations by silencing required alarms or altering standard
safety protocols would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s health and
safety operations. The purpose of these audible warnings is to prevent serious injury or
death. Removing or modifying them for a particular individual would materially change
how the City conducts abatement operations and would introduce foreseeable safety
risks to workers, contractors, bystanders, and the individual himself. The ADA does not
require a public entity to implement modifications that fundamentally alter the nature of
a program or compromise essential safety requirements.
The ADA also does not require the City to purchase or provide personal devices as an
accommodation in these circumstances. Nonetheless, as a discretionary, one-time
measure the City is willing to make available an existing noise reducing, earmuff style
headset for Mr. Coon’s voluntary use during this abatement. This item is being offered
solely because it is already available to the City and presents a reasonable alternative
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 63 of 165

You request an immediate in-person meeting including HRT, Mr. Coon’s medical

4. Temporary Disability Refuge Zone (10×10 Safe Zone at Edge of Abatement Area)
No sanctioned campsite exists in Berkeley at this time, including a designated 10×10
“disability refuge zone”. The City’s encampment regulations generally prohibit
structures exceeding nine square feet on sidewalks and prohibit lodging or storage of
property in certain other locations, including medians and City building curtilage. In
limited circumstances, individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not
exceeding nine square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained and the area is
not designated as a no-lodging zone. Even then, all individuals remain subject to
enforcement based on health and safety conditions.
Designating a specific exemption area for Mr. Coon within the abatement zone, or
allowing him to remain in his current location for an indefinite amount of time until
housing is secured, would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s enforcement
framework. The City must address conditions at this encampment site, including those
associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing
continued occupation, whether indefinitely or beyond the posted deadline, would
interfere with the City’s ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to
public health and safety.
Following posted signage and applicable codes and regulations when relocating will
reduce the likelihood of enforcement in a subsequent location, although enforcement
decisions remain condition-dependent.
5. Bridge Housing on City-Owned Lot
No sanctioned campsite exists at this time, including a temporary site specific to

## ER-723

Mr. Coon. As you’ve described “bridge housing on a City-owned lot” would require the
creation of a new program involving land designation, sanitation infrastructure,
provisions for security, management, and regulatory adjustments. The ADA does not
require the creation of new programs or housing sites as an accommodation.
As an alternative, Mr. Coon is encouraged to engage with HRT regarding currently
available shelter or housing navigation services, or to contact 211 for regional resource
information.
6. Phased Relocation Plan with Peer Support
The City does not offer peer-support services and does not operate a program to develop
individualized, step-by-step relocation schedules for members of the public. The ADA
requires reasonable modifications to existing policies, practices, or procedures where
necessary to avoid discrimination on the basis of disability; it does not require a public
entity to create entirely new programs or provide services of a personal nature.
Further, providing individualized peer-support navigation or step-by-step relocation
planning would constitute the provision of personal services and the creation of a new
programmatic offering, rather than a modification of an existing policy. Such a
requirement would therefore amount to a fundamental alteration of the City’s programs
and exceed the scope of the ADA’s reasonable modification mandate. The type of peer
support described is commonly provided by disability advocacy organizations.
Mr. Coon may wish to contact the Center for Independent Living (CIL) for that assistance
at (510) 841-4776 or info@thecil.org. 7. ADA-Exempted 10×10 Footprint (100 Square Feet)
The request for a permanent 10×10 (100 square foot) footprint on any public sidewalk,
provided a four-foot pedestrian clearance is maintained, cannot be granted. Public
sidewalks serve multiple legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel (and
associated accessibility compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial
activity conducted under active City permits, and placement of utilities and public
infrastructure. Sidewalk widths vary considerably, and in many locations a four-foot
clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once curb ramps, turning radii,
and other obstructions are considered.
Granting a blanket exemption from the nine-square-foot limitation would fundamentally
alter the City’s management of the public right of way and conflict with other legally
protected uses. For those reasons, this request cannot be approved.
Final Determination

Hi Mr. Gregory,
I am writing on behalf of Erik Alexander Johnson Coon, a disabled Berkeley Homeless
Union member who is currently living in a tent/structure at the Harrison Corridor, to
formally submit a request for reasonable accommodations under the Americans with
Disabilities Act (ADA).
1. Disability Verification
Mr. Coon’s disabilities have been medically verified. His healthcare provider, Olivia De
Bree, has already submitted a medical verification letter confirming his conditions,
including:
Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD) resulting from his military service in the
Gulf War (Kuwait, 1993–1997), consistent with Gulf War Syndrome;
Seizure disorder triggered by acute stress and auditory overstimulation (e.g.,
loud sirens or heavy machinery);
Long-standing trauma-related impairments that substantially limit one or more
major life activities, including concentration, interaction with others, and stress
regulation.
2. Nexus Between Disabilities and Requested Accommodations
The following accommodations are requested specifically to help Mr. Coon comply
with the City’s orders, and to do so in a manner that does not exacerbate his
documented disabilities:
a) Safe, Quiet Place to Stay or Relocate To
Due to his PTSD and need for daily structure, Mr. Coon requires either:
A non-congregate ADA-compliant shelter space, or
A clearly designated, safe location where he may remain without harassment or
risk of sudden displacement, particularly during City operations.
Without this, his PTSD symptoms are destabilized, increasing risk of dissociation and
harm. Although the Homeless Response Team (HRT) has been visiting the
encampment throughout the week, they have not offered Mr. Coon any services —
even during times when he was present and visible at his location.
b) Reasonable Modification of Abatement Operations
Mr. Coon’s seizures are triggered by intense noise. To prevent foreseeable harm, we
request that:
Sirens and high-decibel alarms (e.g., from dump trucks and bulldozers) not be
used in proximity to his tent;

## ER-728

Alternative, quieter notice methods or accommodations be used if enforcement
or sanitation activity is required near him.
These steps are essential to prevent both physical and psychological harm directly
connected to his disability.
3. Request for In-Person Meeting
We respectfully request an in-person meeting between the ADA Coordinator, the
HRT team, Berkeley Homeless Union representatives, Mr. Coon and his medical
provider present to discuss these accommodations in detail and ensure a thorough,
individualized, and good-faith interactive process, as required by law.
4. Immediate Next Steps Requested
To fulfill the ADA’s requirements and avoid foreseeable harm to Mr. Coon, we ask the
City to:
1. Pause enforcement activity toward Mr. Coon pending full review of this
request;
2. Engage in the interactive process, including a meeting with Union
representatives;
3. Confirm receipt and next steps within 48 hours, including availability for
scheduling.
We remain ready to work collaboratively and in good faith. Please let us know how best
to move forward with this request.

Hi Yesica, 
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Jacob Clakley.
You have submitted substantially similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals
over the past several months, and you have previously been advised that such requests
would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s operations, impose an undue
burden, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form of reasonable
accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities.
Under Title II of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or
undue burden, because of the impact that a particular request would have on the City
and its programs or services. As I have previously informed you, those impacts to the
City are the same, regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for their request. 
The determinations below, including alternatives where feasible, were made by me in
consultation with the City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and staff.
I personally spoke with Mr. Clakley at the encampment on February 19, 2026. I invited
him to inform me if he had any disabilities that would prevent him from complying with
an order to leave the area. At that time Mr. Clakley informed me of his functional access
needs, and the limitations he described were unrelated to seven of the ten requests that
you make on his behalf. 
Re your first enumerated request (“A Safe Location to Relocate”), no sanctioned
campsite exists in Berkeley at this time. The City’s encampment regulations generally
prohibit structures exceeding nine square feet on sidewalks and prohibit lodging or
storage of property in certain other locations, including medians and City building
curtilage. Individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine
square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained and the area is not designated as
a no-lodging zone. Even then, all individuals remain subject to enforcement based on
health and safety conditions.
Following posted signage and applicable codes and regulations when relocating will
reduce the likelihood of enforcement in a subsequent location, although enforcement
decisions remain condition dependent. If Mr. Clakley is seeking assistance or services,
he may contact HRT or visit https://berkeleyca.gov/safety-health/homeless-services for
information about homeless services that may be available to him.
Re your second enumerated request (“Non-Congregate Shelter Placement”), shelter

capacity, and prioritization criteria. The ADA requires reasonable modifications to
policies to ensure equal access to an existing program; it does not require the creation
of new housing resources or the guarantee of shelter.
Once Mr. Clakley is offered shelter through the City’s Homeless Response Team (HRT),
he may request reasonable modifications related to the shelter environment itself, and
those would be evaluated in that context. Mr. Clakley is encouraged to contact HRT to
learn about currently available non-congregate shelter opportunities. He may also
contact 211 for information regarding regional resources.
Re your third enumerated request (“Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible
Housing”), individuals remain subject to relocation based on health and safety
conditions. Designating a specific exemption area for Mr. Clakley within the abatement
zone, or allowing him to remain in his current location for an indefinite amount of time
until housing is secured, would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s
encampment management program. The City must address conditions at this
encampment site, including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public
health concerns. Allowing indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s ability to
mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety.
Further, designating a specific exemption area for Mr. Clakley for an indefinite amount of
time until housing is secured would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s
encampment management program. The City must address conditions at this
encampment site, including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public
health concerns. Allowing continued indefinite occupation would interfere with the
City’s ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health
and safety. As an alternative, the City will grant Mr. Mr. Clakley an accommodation
to remain through March 10, 2026. The City cannot grant additional time without
causing the fundamental alteration I have explained above.
Re your fourth enumerated request (“Physical Assistance with Relocation”), under
normal circumstances, City staff could help a disabled member of an
encampment physically pack any/all safe and hygienic possessions if the individual is
moving those possessions to a City-sponsored shelter. However, staff currently cannot
sort, bag, or handle these items, even with personal protective equipment, because the
City’s Public Health Officer has determined that, based on recently observed conditions
at the encampment, the belongings are highly likely to be contaminated with Leptospira
through rat urine, rat carcasses, live rats, or mud. Handling such items would pose a
significant public health risk to City staff, constituting an undue administrative burden.

## ER-731

The City has repeatedly conducted direct public health outreach at the encampment
and provided several rounds of notices informing residents of its inability to handle or
store belongings, and it has repeatedly communicated the same information to you, to
try to minimize negative impacts on those remaining at the encampment.
Re your fifth enumerated request (“Storage for Essential Belongings”), the City is
unable to accommodate a request to store belongings. Staff cannot sort, bag, or handle
these items, even with personal protective equipment, because the City’s Public Health
Officer has determined that, based on recently observed conditions at the encampment,
the belongings are highly likely to be contaminated with Leptospira through rat urine, rat
carcasses, live rats, or mud. Handling such items would pose a significant public health
risk to City staff, constituting an undue administrative burden. In addition, the City
cannot store potentially contaminated items, as the Public Health Officer has advised
that Leptospira can survive on contaminated materials for weeks. Requiring the City to
accept such items into storage would impose an undue burden, given the substantial
administrative effort that would be needed either to mitigate those risks or to
decontaminate the storage facility after the items were kept there. This is consistent
with the City’s storage policy that generally excludes the storage of items presenting a
health or safety risk.
Re your sixth enumerated request (“ADA-Exempted Footprint”),the request for an
indefinite or permanent footprint on any public sidewalk, even provided a four-foot
pedestrian clearance is maintained, cannot be granted. Public sidewalks serve multiple
legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel (and associated accessibility
compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial activity conducted under
active City permits, and placement of utilities and public infrastructure. Sidewalk widths
vary considerably, and in many locations a four-foot clearance would not satisfy
accessibility requirements once curb ramps, turning radii, and other obstructions are
considered. Granting Mr. Clakley an exemption from the nine-square-foot limitation
would fundamentally alter the City’s management of the public right-of-way and conflict
with other legally protected uses. For those reasons, this request cannot be approved.
Re your seventh enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice of Any
Enforcement”), the City is unable to grant a request for 30 days’ notice to vacate the
encampment. Providing that extended notice would constitute a fundamental alteration
of the City’s encampment management program because it would prevent the City from
effectively addressing public health hazards, including the presence of Leptospira
identified at the encampment.
The requested 30-day notice would also fundamentally alter the City’s ability to
implement its encampment management program in a way that flexibly responds to
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 73 of 165
ER-731

## ER-732

health and safety conditions in the community. The presence of Leptospira at the
encampment illustrates the types of public health risks that require the City to act
promptly to protect residents, staff, and the broader community. For these reasons, the
City cannot accommodate the requested extended notice period.
Re your eighth enumerated request (“Visual Aid / Map of Permissible
Locations”), the ADA requires public entities to modify policies and procedures when
necessary to ensure that individuals with disabilities have equal access to public
programs. However, the City is not obligated to create individualized maps or preapproved locations that guarantee immunity from enforcement. As previously explained,
the City’s encampment-related requirements are established through a combination of
municipal code provisions, administrative regulations, and City Council resolutions, all
of which are subject to change based on public health conditions, safety concerns, and
legal requirements. Park rules prohibit overnight camping and unattended property
exceeding ten square feet without a permit. The Berkeley Municipal Code and
Administrative Regulation 10.2 generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square feet
on sidewalks and also prohibit lodging or storage of property on medians and within City
building curtilage.
In limited circumstances, individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not
exceeding nine square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained, facilities are not
obstructed, and the location is not designated as a no lodging area. Even in those
circumstances, all individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and
safety impacts, nuisance conditions, and compliance with applicable law. Because
enforcement determinations are location specific, condition dependent, and responsive
to changing circumstances, it is not possible for the City to identify locations where
camping would be categorically free from enforcement.
Further, no map identifying enforcement-free locations exists in City records. Creating a
map that would illustrate areas less likely to draw enforcement would require extensive
staff time, site-specific analysis, and physical measurement of sidewalks and public
spaces throughout the City and would therefore constitute an undue administrative
burden under Title II. As an alternative, the City has summarized the applicable
regulatory framework to assist in understanding how enforcement decisions are made
but cannot provide individualized guarantees regarding future enforcement outcomes.
Re your ninth enumerated request (“Pace on [of?]Enforcement Due to Disabilities”),
Mr. Clakley is free to comply with the lawfully posted abatement order at the pace that
he chooses. Regarding pausing enforcement, please see my response, supra, to your
third enumerated request.

## ER-733

Re your tenth enumerated request (“Temporary Sanitation Services”), the City has,
in limited circumstances, provided portable restrooms, trash receptacles, and limited
waste services at certain encampments as population-level public health measures.
Decisions about whether to deploy these services depend on site conditions, public
health guidance, operational feasibility, and broader encampment priorities, including
the allocation of limited resources. These services are deployed as part of the City’s
encampment management program and are not provided on an individual basis. Also,
when an encampment is cleared, associated sanitation and waste services are
discontinued.
Providing sanitation services to an individual who remains outside following an
encampment closure would require the City to fundamentally alter how its encampment
management program operates. Under current practice, sanitation resources, such as
portable restrooms, trash receptacles, and waste services, are deployed at certain
active encampments as population-level public health measures based on the number
of people present, site conditions, and available resources. These services are designed
to address conditions at encampment sites, not to operate as individual services.
Extending those services to a single individual after an encampment has been cleared
would shift the program from a site-based public health measure to an individualized
service. Doing so would require the City to fundamentally alter the program and redirect
limited sanitation resources in a way that the program is not structured to support. For
that reason, granting the request would fundamentally alter how the City’s encampment
management program functions.
In addition, providing on-demand individualized sanitation services would effectively
create a new program or service. Title II of the ADA requires reasonable modifications to
existing programs but does not require public entities to create entirely new programs.
The City does, however, contract with community partners that provide sanitation and
related services to individuals in need. For example, Dorothy Day House operates a
drop-in center at its main shelter in downtown Berkeley that is open seven days a week,
year-round. The center offers meals, showers, laundry, clothing, lockers, mail services,
internet access, respite space, housing support, ID and DMV assistance, workforce
training, and referrals for medical and addiction services. More information about
these services is available here: https://www.dorothydayhouse.org/services. The City
also maintains public restroom facilities at various locations throughout the City that are
available for public use.
This message constitutes my final determination regarding Mr. Clakley’s first, second,
third, fourth, fifth, sixth, seventh, eighth, ninth, and tenth enumerated requests for
accommodation in connection with the March 2, 2026 Notice of Encampment Closure,

Union currently residing in a tent on Harrison Street, between 7th and 8th streets, to
formally submit a request for reasonable accommodations.
Please find below the information required to initiate this request:
1. Disability Verification
Mr. Clakley lives with documented, life-threatening disabilities, including:
Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) (2006–present)
Heart problems that could lead to cardiac arrest

## ER-735

These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including
breathing, sleeping, walking, standing for extended periods, lifting, carrying and
regulating physical stress. Due to the severity of his conditions, sudden physical
exertion or acute stress could trigger a life-threatening cardiac event.
Mr. Clakley receives care from Lifelong Medical. We are in the process of obtaining
medical verification from his provider and will forward it to you as soon as it becomes
available. Given the urgency of the enforcement timeline and the life-threatening
nature of his conditions, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process
based on the disabilities self-reported in his signed accommodation request form
(attached), pending receipt of formal verification.
2. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the February
17, 2026 Public Notice—will, if applied to Mr. Clakley without modification,
foreseeably aggravate his disabilities in the following ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
Sudden displacement requires rapid physical exertion—packing, lifting, carrying, and
walking—which Mr. Clakley's COPD and heart problems cannot accommodate. The
physical strain alone could trigger a medical emergency, including cardiac arrest.
Additionally, the acute stress of unexpected enforcement can elevate heart rate and
blood pressure, further endangering his life.
b) Physical demands of relocation
Mr. Clakley cannot lift heavy objects, walk long distances, or engage in sustained
physical activity due to his COPD and heart condition. Requiring him to pack and
move his belongings without assistance would be not only impossible but lifethreatening.
c) 9-square-foot limit on sidewalks
Compliance is physically impossible without abandoning his tent (his medical
equipment and only shelter), mobility aids and essential belongings. The space is too
small for him to rest, store his necessities, or maintain the sanitation his conditions
require.
d) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Mr. Clakley for disability-related inability to comply—such as moving too
slowly, being unable to pack within time limits, or being unable to fit his belongings
into 9 square feet—would constitute discrimination under the ADA. The stress of such
encounters could precipitate a cardiac event.

e) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of his belongings—including medications, medical supplies, and items
essential to daily survival—would be devastating. For someone with life-threatening
conditions, losing access to medications and supplies can have fatal consequences.
f) Forced relocation without an accessible destination
Moving Mr. Clakley from one outdoor location to another does not achieve the City's
stated goal of "moving all people indoors." It merely destabilizes him, disrupts access
to medical care, and exposes him to repeated physical strain and stress—each time
increasing his risk of cardiac arrest.
g) Lack of sanitation services
Mr. Clakley's COPD and heart problems require access to clean water, restroom
facilities, and basic sanitation. Without these, his conditions worsen, and his risk of an
infection and medical crisis increases.
3. Requested Accommodations
Based on Mr. Clakley's completed accommodation request form and the lifethreatening nature of his disabilities, we respectfully request the following
accommodations:
1) A Safe Location to Relocate
Mr. Clakley requires a clearly designated, safe location where he can relocate without
fear of citation, arrest, or property destruction. This location must:
Be accessible to someone with severe respiratory and cardiac limitations
Provide access to basic sanitation (restroom, clean water, trash pick up)
Be within reasonable distance to social and medical services
Allow him to rest without immediate threat of further displacement

A hotel room or other private, low-stimulation shelter unit with a private bathroom
where Mr. Clakley can manage his medical needs with dignity. Congregate settings
are medically inappropriate as they increase risk of respiratory infection and provide
inadequate sanitation.

—with at least 30 days' advance notice—to allow him time to:

From: Gregory, Thomas To: Seals, Celestine Cc: De Genova, Haley
Subject: Fw: REQUEST: ADA Accommodation for Jason Charette Date: Monday, March 09, 2026 12:31:58 PM

From: Gregory, Thomas <TGregory@berkeleyca.gov>

Subject: Re: REQUEST: ADA Accommodation for Jason Charette
Hi Yesica,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Jason Charette. You have submitted substantially similar requests on
behalf of numerous individuals over the past several months, and you have previously been advised that such
requests would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s operations, impose an undue burden, are based
on financial considerations rather than disability-related needs, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form
of reasonable accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities. Under Title II of
the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or undue burden because of the impact that
a particular request would have on the City and its programs or services. As I have previously informed you, those
impacts to the City are the same regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for their request. These
determinations were made by me in consultation with the City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and
staff.
Regarding your first enumerated request (“A Safe Location to Relocate”), the City’s “no lodging” prohibitions
do not apply to vehicles or RVs that are lawfully parked. Vehicles remain subject to standard parking enforcement
rules applicable citywide. Any required movement of vehicles or RVs is directed through applicable parking
regulations or temporary signage. Mr. Charette is free to park on Berkeley streets, the same as any other visitor or
resident, in compliance with the California Vehicle Code and posted signage.
The City’s current parking enforcement practices rely primarily on automated license plate recognition
(“ALPR”) technology to identify abandoned vehicles and parking violations. ALPR data is not retained
beyond a limited period, which makes it infeasible to track compliance with extended or individualized
parking timelines. As a result, accommodating an individualized parking exemption would require a
fundamental alteration of standard enforcement practices.
Specifically, parking enforcement staff would be required to rely on manual and labor-intensive methods,
such as maintaining paper logs of protected vehicles, physically marking vehicles, returning to inspect
those markings, and resolving disputes regarding whether a vehicle has moved. These manual methods lack
the permanence, reliability, and data integrity of ALPR-based enforcement and in other contexts have led to
increased disputes with members of the public regarding compliance.
Further, requiring parking and law enforcement officers to remain continually aware of specific vehicles
subject to individualized rules would significantly complicate routine enforcement operations and divert
limited resources from the City’s broader parking, traffic, and safety responsibilities. For these reasons, the
City concludes that granting the requested exemption would impose an undue administrative burden.
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 83 of 165
ER-741
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (106 of 289)

## ER-742

Regarding your second enumerated request (“Non-Congregate Shelter Placement”), shelter placement
is not itself a reasonable accommodation under Title II of the ADA. The provision of shelter is a public
service that may be available depending on eligibility, capacity, and prioritization criteria. The ADA requires
reasonable modifications to policies to ensure equal access to an existing program; it does not require the
creation of new housing resources or the guarantee of a specific housing outcome.
Once Mr. Charette is offered shelter through the City’s Homeless Response Team (HRT), he may request
reasonable modifications related to the shelter environment itself, and those would be evaluated in that
context. If Mr. Charette is seeking assistance or services, he may contact HRT or visit
https://berkeleyca.gov/safety-health/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may
be available to him. Mr. Charette is encouraged to contact HRT to learn about currently available noncongregate shelter opportunities. He may also contact 211 for information regarding regional resources.
Regarding your third enumerated request (“Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing”), all
individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and safety conditions. Designating a specific
exemption area for Mr. Charette within the abatement zone, or allowing him to remain in his current location
for an indefinite amount of time until housing is secured, would constitute a fundamental alteration of the
City’s enforcement framework. The City must address conditions at this encampment site, including those
associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing continued occupation,
whether indefinitely or beyond the posted deadline, would interfere with the City’s ability to mitigate those
risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety. As an alternative, the City will grant Mr.
Charette an accommodation to remain through March 10, 2026.
Regarding your fourth enumerated request (“Recognition of RV as Medical Equipment”), as I have
informed you in the past, reasonable accommodations under the ADA involve modifications to a public
entity’s policies, practices, or procedures. The City does not maintain a policy that classifies vehicles or
recreational vehicles as any particular type of equipment, and therefore there is no City policy applicable to
this request that could be modified through the reasonable accommodation process. Classifications such
as “recreational vehicle” are established by state and federal law, including vehicle registration and
regulatory frameworks under the California Vehicle Code, not by municipal designation. Because the City
does not determine how vehicles are classified under those legal frameworks, it cannot issue a formal
acknowledgment that Mr. Charette’s RV constitutes medical equipment. Additionally, the request does not
identify a nexus between Mr. Charette’s disabilities and any modification to a City policy, program, or
procedure.
Regarding your fifth enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement”), the City is
unable to grant a request for 30 days’ notice to vacate the encampment. Providing that extended notice
would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program because it
would prevent the City from effectively addressing public health hazards, including the presence of
Leptospira identified at the encampment. As part of its ongoing encampment management program, the
City has repeatedly provided both written and verbal notices regarding the need to relocate since January
2025. Your email below cites that Mr. Charette has been on notice since at least February 17, 2026, over
two weeks ago, which the City has determined is sufficient, to the extent he requires additional time to
comply.
Regarding your sixth enumerated request (“Visual Aid / Map of Permissible RV Locations”), visual
signage throughout the City already makes clear where vehicles (including RVs) may park and for how long
they may park there. The City does not maintain a map of parking signage throughout the City and could not
do so, as parking signage often changes on a weekly basis. Creating such a map would present an undue
administrative burden for these reasons.
Regarding your seventh enumerated request (“Exemption from RV Towing and Impoundment”), exempting Mr. Charette’s vehicle from the enforcement of parking rules would result in a fundamental
alteration of the City’s parking enforcement program. The City’s enforcement of local and State provisions
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 84 of 165
ER-742
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (107 of 289)

## ER-743

governing parking is designed to promote public safety, ensure access to limited on-street parking, facilitate
street maintenance, and mitigate neighborhood impacts associated with long-term vehicle storage.
Exempting Mr. Charette’s vehicle from towing would undermine and fundamentally alter the purpose and
application of these enforcement provisions and interfere with the City’s ability to manage community
impacts associated with unlawfully parked vehicles. Mr. Charette’s vehicle may be towed if it is out of
compliance with California registration requirements, including if registration has been expired for more
than six months, as authorized under California Vehicle Code section 22651(o). The City is unable to grant
the request for an exemption. The basis for the request is financial, rather than related to a disability, and
therefore does not qualify as a reasonable accommodation under the ADA. The California Vehicle Code
permits law enforcement to tow vehicles with registration expired for more than six months, and the City
enforces these requirements consistently to ensure public safety and compliance with state law. The City
strives to be flexible with unhoused community members regardless of its authority to tow, which is evident
in that Mr. Charette and several other members of the encampment have been allowed to park unregistered
vehicles around the City for years without being towed, provided they did not cause negative community
impacts.
Regarding your eighth enumerated request (“ADA-Exempted Footprint”), the request for an indefinite or
permanent footprint on any public sidewalk, even provided a four-foot pedestrian clearance is maintained,
cannot be granted. Public sidewalks serve multiple legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel
(and associated accessibility compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial activity conducted
under active City permits, and placement of utilities and public infrastructure. Sidewalk widths vary
considerably, and in many locations a four-foot clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once
curb ramps, turning radii, and other obstructions are considered. Granting Mr. Charette an exemption from
the nine-square-foot limitation would fundamentally alter the City’s management of the public right of way
and conflict with other legally protected uses. For those reasons, this request cannot be approved.
If Mr. Charette and BHU feel an in-person meeting would be useful, I can be available to meet on 3/6 at
3pm. I have limited availability beyond that, given the shortened timeline associated with this abatement.
This message constitutes the City’s final determination regarding the accommodation requests submitted
on Mr. Charette’s behalf. If Mr. Charette disagrees with this determination, he may choose to file a
grievance through the City’s ADA grievance:
https://berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ADA%20Grievance%20Procedure%20and%20Form%20-

## ER-744

I am writing on behalf of Jason Charette, a disabled U.S. military veteran and member of the Berkeley Homeless
Union currently residing in an RV on Harrison Street, to formally submit a request for reasonable
accommodations.
Please find below the information required to initiate this request:
1. Disability Verification
Mr. Charette lives with documented disabilities including:
Clinical Depression (2006–present)
Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD) (2006–present)
These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including sleeping, concentrating, interacting
with others, regulating emotions and handling stress. Mr. Charette has had disabilities for nearly two decades.
Mr. Charette receives care from Kaiser and Lifelong Medical. We are in the process of obtaining medical
verification from his provider and will forward it to you as soon as it becomes available. Given the urgency of the
enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process based on the disabilities selfreported in his signed accommodation request form (attached), pending receipt of formal verification.
2. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the February 17, 2026 Public Notice—will,
if applied to Mr. Charette without modification, foreseeably aggravate his disabilities in the following ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
His PTSD and clinical depression require predictability and stability. Sudden displacement triggers
hypervigilance, anxiety and emotional dysregulation, leaving him unable to plan or comply with City orders.
b) Loss of RV as medical equipment
For Mr. Charette, his RV is not merely personal property—it is essential medical equipment that provides:
A private, locked space where he can manage PTSD symptoms without fear of intrusion;
A controlled environment that minimizes stress triggers;
Access to basic sanitation (bathroom) and nutrition (kitchen) , which are critical for managing depression
and maintaining daily function;
The stability and predictability his disabilities require;
Removing him from his RV without a suitable, accessible alternative would constitute denial of medical necessity
and would foreseeably aggravate his mental health disabilities.
c) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Mr. Charette for disability-related inability to comply—such as moving his RV without an accessible
and end-point destination—would constitute discrimination under the ADA. The trauma of enforcement actions
and towing his RV can deepen PTSD and trigger depressive episodes.
d) Forced relocation without an accessible destination
Moving Mr. Charette from one location to another without a guaranteed, accessible indoor placement does not
achieve the City's stated goal of "moving all people indoors." It merely destabilizes him, disrupts any connection
to services, and exacerbates his PTSD and depression.
3. Requested Accommodations
Based on Mr. Charette's completed accommodation request form, his status as a disabled veteran, and our
discussions with him, we respectfully request the following accommodations to enable him to comply with City
orders without exacerbating his disabilities:
1) A Safe Location to Relocate

Mr. Charette requires a clearly designated, safe location where he can relocate his RV without fear of citation,

Be large enough to accommodate an RV
Provide access to basic sanitation
Be free from immediate threat of further enforcement
Allow him to maintain the privacy and security his disabilities require
2) Non-Congregate Shelter Placement (with Bathroom, Kitchen, and Locking Door)
If RV relocation is not feasible, Mr. Charette requires a hotel room or other private, low-stimulation shelter unit
that includes:
A locking door for security and privacy (essential for PTSD)
A private bathroom for hygiene and dignity
A kitchen or kitchenette for preparing food and maintaining nutrition
No strict curfews or rigid rules that trigger anxiety and feelings of being trapped
3) Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing
If no suitable relocation site or non-congregate shelter is immediately available, Mr. Charette must be permitted to
remain temporarily in the Harrison Street area until an accessible alternative becomes available. Stability is
medically necessary to prevent aggravation of his PTSD and depression.
4) Recognition of RV as Medical Equipment
A formal acknowledgment that Mr. Charette's RV constitutes medical equipment and will not be towed,
impounded, or destroyed during any enforcement action. If relocation is required, the RV must be permitted to
move intact to a designated safe location.
5) Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement
Due to his PTSD and depression, Mr. Charette requires advance written notice of any enforcement, cleaning, or
relocation activity affecting his location—with sufficient time to process the information and prepare emotionally.
We request a minimum of 30 days' advance written notice.
6) Visual Aid / Map of Permissible RV Locations
A simple, visual map, diagram, list, or document showing where RVs may legally park within the city, including
areas where enforcement is paused or imminent. This accommodates his disability-related need for clarity and
predictability.
7) Exemption from RV Towing and Impoundment
Mr. Charette's RV serves as his home and medical equipment. Towing it would:
Render him immediately unsheltered without any accessible alternative
Separate him from his essential belongings and source of stability
Trigger acute PTSD symptoms and depressive episodes
Violate his rights under the ADA by punishing disability-related needs
If relocation is required, we request that Mr. Charette is permitted to drive his RV to a designated safe location
rather than having it towed and impounded.
8) ADA-Exempted Footprint
A permanent accommodation allowing Mr. Charette to maintain a footprint larger than the standard 9-square-foot
limit—sufficient to accommodate his mobility tools and his essential belongings—next to his RV.
4. Request for Interactive Process Meeting
We respectfully request an in-person meeting with the ADA Coordinator, HRT, Mr. Charette, a Union
representative and his medical provider to discuss these accommodations in detail and ensure a thorough,
individualized interactive process.
