## ER-788

Re your fourth enumerated request (“ADA-Exempted Footprint”), the request for an indefinite or
permanent footprint on any public sidewalk, even provided a four-foot pedestrian clearance is maintained,
cannot be granted. Public sidewalks serve multiple legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel
(and associated accessibility compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial activity conducted
under active City permits, and placement of utilities and public infrastructure. Sidewalk widths vary
considerably, and in many locations a four-foot clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once
curb ramps, turning radii, and other obstructions are considered. Granting Shawman an exemption from the
nine-square-foot limitation would fundamentally alter the City’s management of the public right-of-way and
conflict with other legally protected uses. For those reasons, this request cannot be approved.
Re your fifth enumerated request (“Direct Point of Contact”), Shawman may contact me at (510) 981-6418
and/or ada@cityofberkeley.info if they have questions.
Re your sixth enumerated request (“Visual Aid on Relocation Options”), the ADA requires public entities to
modify policies and procedures when necessary to ensure that individuals with disabilities have equal access
to public programs. However, the City is not obligated to create individualized maps or pre-approved locations
that guarantee immunity from enforcement. As previously explained, the City’s encampment-related
requirements are established through a combination of municipal code provisions, administrative regulations,
and City Council resolutions, all of which are subject to change based on public health conditions, safety
concerns, and legal requirements. Park rules prohibit overnight camping and unattended property exceeding
ten square feet without a permit. The Berkeley Municipal Code and Administrative Regulation 10.2 generally
prohibit structures exceeding nine square feet on sidewalks and also prohibit lodging or storage of property on
medians and within City building curtilage.
In limited circumstances, individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine
square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained, facilities are not obstructed, and the location is not
designated as a no lodging area. Even in those circumstances, all individuals remain subject to enforcement
based on health and safety impacts, nuisance conditions, and compliance with applicable law. Because
enforcement determinations are location specific, condition dependent, and responsive to changing
circumstances, it is not possible for the City to identify locations where camping would be categorically free
from enforcement.
Further, no map identifying enforcement-free locations exists in City records. Creating a map that would
illustrate areas less likely to draw enforcement would require extensive staff time, site-specific analysis, and
physical measurement of sidewalks and public spaces throughout the City and would therefore constitute an
undue administrative burden under Title II. As an alternative, the City has summarized the applicable
regulatory framework to assist in understanding how enforcement decisions are made but cannot provide
individualized guarantees regarding future enforcement outcomes.
Re your seventh enumerated request (“Step-by-Step Guidance”), the steps that Shawman takes to comply
with the abatement order, as well as the sequence of such steps, are up to Shawman. If Shawman seeks
step-by-step guidance, perhaps BHU can offer him guidance. I recommend: (1) gather all possessions not
being abandoned, then (2) remove self and unabandoned possessions from the posted abatement zone.
Re your eighth enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement”), the City is unable to
grant a request for 30 days’ notice to vacate the encampment. Providing that extended notice would
constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program because it would
prevent the City from effectively addressing public health hazards, including the presence of Leptospira
identified at the encampment.
The requested 30-day notice would also fundamentally alter the City’s ability to implement its encampment
management program in a way that flexibly responds to health and safety conditions in the community. The
presence of Leptospira at the encampment illustrates the types of public health risks that require the City to
act promptly to protect residents, staff, and the broader community. For these reasons, the City cannot
accommodate the requested extended notice period.
Re your ninth enumerated request (“Physical Assistance with Relocation”), under normal circumstances,
City staff could help a disabled member of an encampment physically pack and transport safe and hygienic
possessions if the individual is moving those possessions to a City-sponsored shelter. However, staff
currently cannot sort, bag, or handle such items, even with personal protective equipment, because the City’s
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 130 of 165

## ER-789

Public Health Officer has determined that, based on recently observed conditions at the encampment, the
belongings are highly likely to be contaminated with Leptospira through rat urine, rat carcasses, live rats, or
mud. Handling such items would pose a significant public health risk to City staff, constituting an undue
administrative burden.
Re your tenth enumerated request (“Sanitation Services + Water Access”), the City has, in limited
circumstances, provided portable restrooms, trash receptacles, and limited waste services at certain
encampments as population-level public health measures. Decisions about whether to deploy these services
depend on site conditions, public health guidance, operational feasibility, and broader encampment
priorities, including the allocation of limited resources. These services are deployed as part of the City’s
encampment management program and are not provided on an individual basis. Also, when an encampment
is cleared, associated sanitation and waste services are discontinued.
Providing sanitation services to an individual who remains outside following an encampment closure would
require the City to fundamentally alter how its encampment management program operates. Under current
practice, sanitation resources, such as portable restrooms, trash receptacles, and waste services, are
deployed at certain active encampments as population-level public health measures based on the number of
people present, site conditions, and available resources. These services are designed to address conditions at
encampment sites, not to operate as individual services.
Extending those services to a single individual after an encampment has been cleared would shift the program
from a site-based public health measure to an individualized service. Doing so would require the City to
fundamentally alter the program and redirect limited sanitation resources in a way that the program is not
structured to support. For that reason, granting the request would fundamentally alter how the City’s
encampment management program functions.
In addition, providing on-demand individualized sanitation services would effectively create a new program or
service. Title II of the ADA requires reasonable modifications to existing programs but does not require public
entities to create entirely new programs.
The City does, however, contract with community partners that provide sanitation and related services to
individuals in need. For example, Dorothy Day House operates a drop-in center at its main shelter in
downtown Berkeley that is open seven days a week, year-round. The center offers meals, beverages (including
water), showers, laundry, clothing, lockers, mail services, internet access, respite space, housing support, ID
and DMV assistance, workforce training, and referrals for medical and addiction services. More information
about these services is available here: https://www.dorothydayhouse.org/services. The City also maintains
public restroom facilities and public water fountains at various locations throughout the City that are available
for public use.
Re your eleventh enumerated request (“Low-Stimulation Meetings”), the City is not requiring Shawman to
attend any meetings. Whenever Shawman arranges a meeting with a City staff person, they are welcome to
contact me to explain the specific requests they have to minimize the possibility that they will become
overstimulated, and I will facilitate those accommodation requests as best as possible for each meeting,
consistent with my duties as the City’s ADA Coordinator.
This message constitutes my final determination regarding Shawman's first, second, third, fourth, fifth, sixth,
seventh, eighth, ninth, tenth, and eleventh enumerated requests for accommodation in connection with the
March 2, 2026 Notice of Encampment Closure; Shawman's requests are denied on the basis described above,
and due to the fact that the request are not supported by sufficient information showing the disability-related
need, and the requested accommodation would impede actions necessary to protect public health. If
Shawman disagrees with this determination, they may choose to file a grievance through the City’s ADA
grievance:
https://berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/ADA%20Grievance%20Procedure%20and%20Form%20-
%20updated%20Feb2024%20-%20WCAG-compliant.pdf.
If you would still like to request a meeting after reviewing the City’s determination, please share your
availability.
Best,
Thomas Gregory / ADA Program Coordinator

accommodations.
Please find below the information required to initiate this request:
1. Disability Verification
Shawman lives with documented, multiple disabilities resulting from trauma and other medical conditions,
including:
Traumatic Brain Injury: History of being hit in the head with a club, causing:
Internal bleeding
Impaired memory
Executive functioning issues
Physical Disabilities:
Vision problems 
Hearing impairment in one ear (left), which contributes to spatial awareness difficulties.
Mental Health Disabilities:
Severe anxiety
Panic attacks

## ER-791

Other mental health conditions stemming from abuse and trauma
These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including remembering, concentrating,
decision-making, task initiation and completion, seeing, hearing, regulating emotions and handling stress.
Her executive functioning impairment means she cannot independently structure and complete the
complex tasks required for relocation without direct, step-by-step support.
Shawman receives care from Alta Bates Hospital and the Lifelong Medical Street Team. We are in the
process of obtaining medical verification from her providers and will forward it to you as soon as it
becomes available. Given the urgency of the enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the
interactive process based on the disabilities self-reported in her signed accommodation request form
(attached), pending receipt of formal verification.
2. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the March 2, 2026 Public Notice—
will, if applied to Shawman without modification, foreseeably aggravate her disabilities in the following
ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
Her traumatic brain injury, impaired memory, and executive dysfunction require predictability and adequate
time to process information. Sudden displacement triggers confusion, disorientation, panic attacks and
complete task paralysis, leaving her unable to plan or comply with City orders.
b) Physical demands of relocation
Due to her executive functioning impairment, vision problems and panic attacks, Shawman cannot
independently organize, pack, or transport her belongings. The physical tasks of relocation are impossible
without direct, step-by-step assistance because she cannot structure the work, remember what to do, or
follow through without support.
c) 9-square-foot limit on sidewalks
Compliance is impossible without abandoning her tent (her medical equipment and only shelter) and
essential personal belongings. Her executive dysfunction prevents her from making rapid decisions about
what to keep or discard, and her vision and hearing impairments make navigating a new environment
dangerous.
d) Panic attacks triggered by enforcement activity
The stress of enforcement encounters—loud noises, unfamiliar officials, sudden demands—directly
triggers Shawman's panic attacks, which can be debilitating and leave her in a state of shock unable to
function or comply.
e) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Shawman for disability-related inability to comply—such as being unable to organize her
belongings, pack within time limits, or make decisions under pressure—would constitute discrimination
under the ADA. The stress of such encounters would trigger panic attacks and deepen her trauma.
f) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of her belongings—including items essential to daily survival—would be devastating. For
someone with traumatic brain injury, memory impairment, and anxiety, losing familiar, essential items can
trigger acute psychological crisis and further cognitive decline.
g) Vision and hearing impairments

## ER-792

Shawman's vision problems and hearing impairment in her left ear mean that standard written notices may
be difficult for her to read, and verbal instructions may be difficult for her to hear or understand, especially
in stressful enforcement environments. She has been completely unaware all this time of the notices that
have been posted for the encampment closure.
3. Requested Accommodations
Based on Shawman's completed accommodation request form and her multiple disabilities, we respectfully
request the following accommodations:
1) Non-Congregate Shelter Placement
Shawman requires a hotel room or other private, low-stimulation shelter unit with specific modifications: 
Peer support available to help her navigate the shelter environment
A private bathroom
No strict curfews or rigid rules that trigger anxiety and panic
Allow visitors to her room
Mental Health support and counseling
2) Relocation to a City-owned Lot
If shelter is unavailable, Shawman requests relocation to a City-owned lot where she can camp safely with:
A footprint larger than 9 square feet for her dwelling
Access to basic sanitation services such as porta potties, trash pick-up and water
Freedom from immediate threat of further enforcement
3) Temporary Shelter-in-Place at Harrison Street
If no suitable relocation option is immediately available, Shawman must be permitted to remain temporarily
in the Harrison Street area until an alternative is secured. Stability is medically necessary to prevent
aggravation of her traumatic brain injury symptoms, memory impairment, and anxiety.
4) ADA-Exempted Footprint
A permanent accommodation allowing Shawman to maintain a tent footprint larger than the standard 9-
square-foot limit—sufficient to accommodate herself and her essential belongings—on any public sidewalk
where:
She is not blocking pedestrian access, meaning a clear path remains for others to pass safely; and

A simple, visual map, diagram, list, or document showing where she may legally relocate within the city,

Process the information without triggering panic
Work through each step with her point of contact
Prepare her belongings at a pace that accommodates her disabilities

Hi Yesica, 
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Thomas Barnett. I have reviewed this request in light of the March
2, 2026 Notice of Encampment Closure and in consultation with the City Manager and relevant departmental
staff. I acknowledge that the March 2, 2026 Notice of Encampment Closure establishes that enforcement
activity is imminent in the defined geographic area. The City has therefore evaluated these requests in the
context of a pending action. I have assessed each requested accommodation and identified alternatives
where feasible. I personally spoke with Mr. Barnett at the encampment on February 19, 2026, and he informed
me that the only accommodation he would need is assistance relocating his belongings. That request, along
with these additional requests submitted by you, are addressed below.
Despite your assertion that the City has provided “short notice,” the City has repeatedly provided notice of the
need to vacate the area and conducted direct outreach to individuals at the encampment, since January 2025,
including my recent discussion with Mr. Barnett two weeks ago. Further, you have submitted substantially
similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals over the past several months, and you have previously
been advised that such requests would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s operations, impose
an undue burden, are based on financial considerations rather than disability-related needs, or, in the case of
shelter, do not constitute a form of reasonable accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular
individual’s disabilities. Under Title II of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration
or undue burden because of the impact that a particular request would have on the City and its programs or
services. As I have previously informed you, those impacts to the City are the same regardless of the
individual’s disability or the nexus for their request.
Regarding your first enumerated request (“A Safe Location to Relocate”), no sanctioned campsite exists in
Berkeley at this time. The City’s encampment regulations generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square
feet on sidewalks and prohibit lodging or storage of property in certain other locations, including medians and
City building curtilage. Individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine square
feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained and the area is not designated as a no-lodging zone. Even then,
all individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and safety conditions.
Following posted signage and applicable codes and regulations when relocating will reduce the likelihood of
enforcement in a subsequent location, although enforcement decisions remain condition dependent. If Mr.

## ER-798

Barnett is seeking assistance or services, he may contact HRT or visit https://berkeleyca.gov/safetyhealth/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may be available to him.
Regarding your second enumerated request (“Non-Congregate Shelter Placement”), shelter placement is
not itself a reasonable accommodation under Title II of the ADA. The provision of shelter is a public service
that may be available depending on eligibility, capacity, and prioritization criteria. The ADA requires
reasonable modifications to policies to ensure equal access to an existing program; it does not require the
creation of new housing resources or the guarantee of shelter.
Once Mr. Barnett is offered shelter through the City’s Homeless Response Team (HRT), he may request
reasonable modifications related to the shelter environment itself, and those would be evaluated in that
context. Mr. Barnett is encouraged to contact HRT to learn about currently available non-congregate shelter
opportunities. He may also contact 211 for information regarding regional resources.
Regarding your third enumerated request (“Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible
Housing”), individuals remain subject to relocation based on health and safety conditions. Designating a
specific exemption area for Mr. Barnett within the abatement zone, or allowing him to remain in his current
location for an indefinite amount of time until housing is secured, would constitute a fundamental alteration
of the City’s encampment management program. The City must address conditions at this encampment site,
including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing
indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct
threat to public health and safety.
Further, designating a specific exemption area for Mr. Barnett for an indefinite amount of time until housing is
secured would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program. The City
must address conditions at this encampment site, including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis
related public health concerns. Allowing continued indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s ability
to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety. As an alternative, the
City will grant Mr. Barnett an accommodation to remain through March 10, 2026. The City cannot grant
additional time without causing the fundamental alteration I have explained above.
Regarding your fourth enumerated request (“Physical Assistance with Relocation”), under normal
circumstances, City staff could help a disabled member of an encampment physically pack and transport
safe and hygienic possessions if the individual is moving those possessions to a City-sponsored shelter.
However, that option is not available due to the reasons I’ve explained above.
Regarding your fifth enumerated request (“Storage for Essential Belongings”), the City is unable to
accommodate a request to store belongings. Public Works staff cannot sort, bag, or handle these items, even
with personal protective equipment, because the City’s Public Health Officer has determined that, based on
recently observed conditions at the encampment, the belongings are highly likely to be contaminated with
Leptospira through rat urine, rat carcasses, live rats, or mud. Handling such items would pose a significant
public health risk to City staff, constituting an undue administrative burden. In addition, the City cannot store
potentially contaminated items, as the Public Health Officer has advised that Leptospira can survive on
contaminated materials for weeks. Requiring the City to accept such items into storage would impose an
undue burden, given the substantial administrative effort that would be needed either to mitigate those risks
or to decontaminate the storage facility after the items were kept there. This is consistent with the City’s
storage policy that generally excludes the storage of items presenting a health or safety risk. The City has
repeatedly conducted direct public health outreach at the encampment and provided several rounds of
notices informing residents of its inability to handle or store belongings, and it has repeatedly communicated
the same information to you, to try to minimize negative impacts on those remaining at the encampment.

cannot be granted. Public sidewalks serve multiple legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel
(and associated accessibility compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial activity conducted
under active City permits, and placement of utilities and public infrastructure. Sidewalk widths vary
considerably, and in many locations a four-foot clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once
curb ramps, turning radii, and other obstructions are considered. Granting Mr. Barnett an exemption from the
nine-square-foot limitation would fundamentally alter the City’s management of the public right-of-way and
conflict with other legally protected uses. For those reasons, this request cannot be approved.
Regarding your seventh enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice of 30 Days for Any
Enforcement”), the City is unable to grant a request for 30 days’ notice to vacate the encampment. Providing
that extended notice would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management
program because it would prevent the City from effectively addressing public health hazards, including the
presence of Leptospira identified at the encampment. As part of its ongoing encampment management
program, the City has repeatedly provided both written and verbal notices regarding the need to relocate since
January 2025. I personally spoke with Mr. Barnett on February 19, 2026, two weeks ago, to provide verbal
notice, which the City has determined is sufficient, to the extent he requires additional time to comply.
The requested 30-day notice would also fundamentally alter the City’s ability to implement its encampment
management program in a way that flexibly responds to health and safety conditions in the community. The
presence of Leptospira at the encampment illustrates the types of public health risks that require the City to
act promptly to protect residents, staff, and the broader community. For these reasons, the City cannot
accommodate the requested extended notice period.
Regarding your eighth enumerated request (“Visual Aid / Map of Permissible Locations”), the ADA
requires public entities to modify policies and procedures when necessary to ensure that individuals with
disabilities have equal access to public programs. However, the City is not obligated to create individualized
maps or pre-approved locations that guarantee immunity from enforcement. As previously explained, the
City’s encampment-related requirements are established through a combination of municipal code
provisions, administrative regulations, and City Council resolutions, all of which are subject to change based
on public health conditions, safety concerns, and legal requirements. Park rules prohibit overnight camping
and unattended property exceeding ten square feet without a permit. The Berkeley Municipal Code and
Administrative Regulation 10.2 generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square feet on sidewalks and also
prohibit lodging or storage of property on medians and within City building curtilage.
In limited circumstances, individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine
square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained, facilities are not obstructed, and the location is not
designated as a no lodging area. Even in those circumstances, all individuals remain subject to enforcement
based on health and safety impacts, nuisance conditions, and compliance with applicable law. Because
enforcement determinations are location specific, condition dependent, and responsive to changing
circumstances, it is not possible for the City to identify locations where camping would be categorically free
from enforcement.
Further, no map identifying enforcement-free locations exists in City records. Creating a map that would
illustrate areas less likely to draw enforcement would require extensive staff time, site-specific analysis, and
physical measurement of sidewalks and public spaces throughout the City and would therefore constitute an
undue administrative burden under Title II. As an alternative, the City has summarized the applicable
regulatory framework to assist in understanding how enforcement decisions are made but cannot provide
individualized guarantees regarding future enforcement outcomes.
Regarding your ninth enumerated request (“Designated Point of Contact”), Mr. Barnett may contact me at

ER-799

(510) 981-6418 ada@cityofberkeley.info if he has questions.
Regarding your tenth enumerated request (“Exemption from Vehicle Towing”), Mr. Barnett’s vehicle may
be towed if it is out of compliance with California registration requirements, including if registration has been
expired for more than six months, as authorized under California Vehicle Code ? 22651(o). The City is unable
to grant the request for an exemption. The basis for the request is financial, rather than related to a disability,
and therefore does not qualify as a reasonable accommodation under the ADA. The California Vehicle Code
permits law enforcement to tow vehicles with registration expired for more than six months, and the City
enforces these requirements consistently to ensure public safety and compliance with state law. The City
strives to be flexible with unhoused community members regardless of its authority to tow, which is evident in
that Mr. Barnett and several other members of the encampment have been allowed to park around the City for
years without being towed, provided they did not cause negative community impacts.
If Mr. Barnett and BHU feel an in-person meeting would be useful, I can be available to meet on 3/6 at 2pm.

Mr. Barnett’s behalf. If Mr. Barnett disagrees with this determination, he may choose to file a grievance
through the City’s ADA grievance:

Hi Mr. Gregory and Homeless Response Team,
I am writing on behalf of Thomas Barnett, a disabled member of the Berkeley Homeless Union currently
residing in a tent/structure/vehicle at the west corner of 8th Street and Harrison Street, to formally submit a
request for reasonable accommodations.
Please find below the information required to initiate this request.
1. Disability Verification
Mr. Barnett lives with documented disabilities, including:
Physical Disabilities:
Limited mobility in his right arm and hand, which prevents him from lifting heavy objects
Mobility impairment on his legs, as he cannot walk long distances
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 142 of 165
ER-800
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (165 of 289)

## ER-801

Mental Health Disabilities:
Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD)
Short-term memory impairment
Dementia (early stage)
These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including walking, lifting, carrying,
concentrating, remembering, processing information and regulating emotions.
Mr. Barnett receives care from the Lifelong Street Medicine Team. We are in the process of obtaining
medical verification from his provider and will forward it to you as soon as it becomes available. Given the
urgency of the enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process based on
the disabilities self-reported in his signed accommodation request form (attached), pending receipt of
formal verification.
2. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the February 17, 2026 Public
Notice—will, if applied to Mr. Barnett without modification, foreseeably aggravate his disabilities in the
following ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
His PTSD, short-term memory impairment, and dementia require predictability and adequate time to
process information. Sudden displacement triggers confusion, disorientation, and emotional dysregulation,
leaving him unable to plan or comply with City orders.
b) Physical demands of relocation
Due to his limited mobility in his right arm and hand, Mr. Barnett cannot lift heavy objects. His mobility
impairment prevents him from walking long distances. Requiring him to pack and move his belongings
without physical assistance would be impossible and would cause significant physical strain and risk of
injury.
c) 9-square-foot limit on sidewalks
Compliance is physically impossible without abandoning his tent (his only shelter), mobility tools, and
essential belongings. His physical disabilities prevent him from carrying items to another location, and the
space is too small for him to rest or store his necessities.
d) Threat of citation, arrest, and property destruction
Penalizing Mr. Barnett for disability-related inability to comply—such as moving too slowly, being unable to
pack within time limits, or being unable to lift or carry his belongings—would constitute discrimination under
the ADA.
e) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of his belongings—including his mobility tools and items essential to daily survival—would be
devastating. For someone with PTSD and dementia, losing familiar, essential items can trigger acute
psychological crises and further cognitive decline.
f) Forced relocation without an accessible destination
Moving Mr. Barnett from one outdoor location to another does not achieve the City's stated goal of "moving
all people indoors." It merely destabilizes him, disrupts any connection to services, and exacerbates his
PTSD and cognitive impairments.
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 143 of 165
ER-801
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (166 of 289)

## ER-802

3. Requested Accommodations
To enable Mr. Barnett to comply with any City enforcement or relocation orders without exacerbating his
disabilities, we respectfully request the following accommodations:
1) A Safe Location to Relocate
Mr. Barnett requires a clearly designated, safe location where he can relocate without fear of citation,
arrest, or property destruction. This location must be accessible to someone with mobility impairments and
within reasonable distance to services.
2) Non-Congregate Shelter Placement
A hotel room or other private, low-stimulation shelter unit—without strict curfews—where Mr. Barnett can
maintain his independence and sense of safety. Strict curfews and rigid rules are contraindicated for
individuals with PTSD and dementia, as they can trigger anxiety, confusion and feelings of being trapped.
Congregate settings are medically inappropriate.
3) Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing
If no non-congregate shelter is immediately available, Mr. Barnett must be permitted to remain temporarily
in the 8th & Harrison area until an accessible room becomes available. Stability is medically necessary to
prevent aggravation of his PTSD and cognitive decline.
4) Physical Assistance with Relocation
Due to his limited mobility in his right arm and hand and his inability to walk long distances, Mr. Barnett
requires City staff assistance with:
Lifting and carrying heavy items
Transporting his mobility tools and essential belongings to any new location
This assistance should be scheduled in advance and coordinated with his needs.
5) Storage for Essential Belongings
We acknowledge the City's concern regarding leptospirosis. However, we request that the City identify a
safe storage option for Mr. Barnett's essential items—particularly his work tools and items necessary for
daily survival. If storage is impossible, we request advance notice and assistance to transport his
belongings to another safe location.
6) ADA-Exempted Footprint
A permanent accommodation allowing Mr. Barnett to maintain a tent footprint larger than the standard 9-
square-foot limit—sufficient to accommodate himself, his mobility tools and his essential belongings—on
any public sidewalk where:
He is not blocking pedestrian access, meaning a clear path remains for others to pass safely; and
If located on a narrower sidewalk, the opposite side of the street remains clear for alternative
pedestrian access.
This modification recognizes that for Mr. Barnett, his tent functions as his only accessible shelter and as
medical equipment.
7) Advance Written Notice of 30 Days for Any Enforcement
Due to his short-term memory impairment and early stage of dementia, Mr. Barnett requires advance
written notice of any enforcement, cleaning, or relocation activity affecting his location—with at least one
follow-up reminder—to allow him time to process the information and prepare. Verbal instructions alone
are insufficient and will be forgotten. We request a minimum of 30 days' advance written notice.
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 144 of 165
ER-802
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (167 of 289)

## ER-803

8) Visual Aid / Map of Permissible Locations
A simple, visual map, diagram, list or document showing where he may legally relocate within the city,
including areas where tents are permitted. This accommodates his cognitive disabilities and memory
impairment.
9) Designated Point of Contact
A single, consistent point of contact from HRT or the ADA Coordinator to help Mr. Barnett navigate the
relocation process. Frequent changes in staff or messengers will cause confusion and memory loss.
10) Exemption from Vehicle Towing
Mr. Barnett's vehicle serves a critical disability-related function and should be exempt from towing and
impoundment. Specifically, the vehicle:
Stores essential belongings that Mr. Barnett cannot lift or carry due to his mobility impairments,
including his work tools, clothing, bedding and medical supplies.
Prevents the need for a larger sidewalk footprint, as he uses the vehicle to store items that would
otherwise require additional tent space.
Provides an alternative rest space when his tent is insufficient or during extreme weather.
Towing his vehicle would:
Render his essential belongings inaccessible and likely result in their loss;
Force him to store those items in his tent, requiring a larger footprint and increasing the likelihood of
citation;
Eliminate a critical accommodation that helps him manage his disabilities with greater independence;
If relocation is required, we request that Mr. Barnett be permitted to move his vehicle to a designated safe
location rather than having it towed and impounded.
4. Request for Interactive Process Meeting
We respectfully request an in-person meeting with the ADA Coordinator, HRT, Mr. Barnett, a Union
representative, and his medical provider (Lifelong Street Medicine Team) to discuss these
accommodations in detail and ensure a thorough, individualized interactive process.
5. Immediate Next Steps Requested
To fulfill the ADA's requirements and avoid foreseeable harm to Mr. Barnett, we ask the City to:
Pause any enforcement activity toward Mr. Barnett pending full review of this request and completion
of the interactive process;
Confirm receipt of this request within 48 hours;
Provide available dates for a meeting;
Provide a substantive response to each of the requested accommodations listed above; and
Suggest alternatives if any of the requested accommodations are deemed not feasible, so that we
can continue the interactive process in good faith.
We ask that the City not delay the interactive process while awaiting medical documentation, given the
imminent enforcement deadline.
Mr. Barnett is ready and willing to engage in good faith. We look forward to working collaboratively to
ensure his disability-related needs are met.

Hi Yesica, 
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Hector Navarro.
You have submitted substantially similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals over the past several
months, and you have previously been advised that such requests would constitute a fundamental alteration
of the City’s operations, impose an undue burden, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form of
reasonable accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities. Under Title II
of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or undue burden, because of the
impact that a particular request would have on the City and its programs or services. As I have previously
informed you, those impacts to the City are the same, regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for
their request. The determinations below, including alternatives where feasible, were made by me in
consultation with the City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and staff.
This message is being provided in English because it was sent in English and is being sent to you, as you have
not provided an email address for Mr. Navarro. If Mr. Navarro would like a copy of this email translated into
Spanish, please let me know as soon as possible and indicate whether he would like a printed copy delivered
to him or prefers to arrange a phone call with me, during which I will provide live interpretation. Mr. Navarro
may also request language interpretation or translation services if he attempts to access the City’s homeless
services listed below.
Re your first enumerated request (“Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing”), individuals
remain subject to relocation based on health and safety conditions. Designating a specific exemption area for
Mr. Navarro within the abatement zone, or allowing him to remain in his current location for an indefinite
amount of time until housing is secured, would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment
management program. The City must address conditions at this encampment site, including those associated
with ongoing leptospirosis related public health concerns. Allowing indefinite occupation would interfere with
the City’s ability to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety.
Further, designating a specific exemption area for Mr. Navarro for an indefinite amount of time until housing is
secured would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s encampment management program. The City
must address conditions at this encampment site, including those associated with ongoing leptospirosis
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 147 of 165
ER-805
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (170 of 289)

## ER-806

related public health concerns. Allowing continued indefinite occupation would interfere with the City’s ability
to mitigate those risks and would present a direct threat to public health and safety. As an alternative, the
City will grant Mr. Navarro an accommodation to remain through March 10, 2026. The City cannot grant
additional time without causing the fundamental alteration I have explained above.
Re your second enumerated request (“A Safe Relocation Option”), no sanctioned campsite exists in
Berkeley at this time. The City’s encampment regulations generally prohibit structures exceeding nine square
feet on sidewalks and prohibit lodging or storage of property in certain other locations, including medians and
City building curtilage. Individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine square
feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained and the area is not designated as a no-lodging zone. Even then,
all individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and safety conditions.
Following posted signage and applicable codes and regulations when relocating will reduce the likelihood of
enforcement in a subsequent location, although enforcement decisions remain condition dependent. If Mr.
Navarro is seeking assistance or services, he may contact HRT or visit https://berkeleyca.gov/safetyhealth/homeless-services for information about homeless services that may be available to him.
Re your third enumerated request (“Non-Congregate Shelter Placement”), shelter placement is not itself a
reasonable accommodation under Title II of the ADA. The provision of shelter is a public service that may be
available depending on eligibility, capacity, and prioritization criteria. The ADA requires reasonable
modifications to policies to ensure equal access to an existing program; it does not require the creation of new
housing resources or the guarantee of shelter.
Once Mr. Navarro is offered shelter through the City’s Homeless Response Team (HRT), he may request
reasonable modifications related to the shelter environment itself, and those would be evaluated in that
context. Mr. Navarro is encouraged to contact HRT to learn about currently available non-congregate shelter
opportunities. He may also contact 211 for information regarding regional resources.
Re your fourth enumerated request (“Physical Assistance with Relocation”), under normal
circumstances, City staff could help a disabled member of an encampment physically pack and transport
safe and hygienic possessions if the individual is moving those possessions to a City-sponsored shelter. 
However, staff currently cannot sort, bag, or handle such items, even with personal protective equipment,
because the City’s Public Health Officer has determined that, based on recently observed conditions at the
encampment, the belongings are highly likely to be contaminated with Leptospira through rat urine, rat
carcasses, live rats, or mud. Handling such items would pose a significant public health risk to City staff,
constituting an undue administrative burden.
Re your fifth enumerated request (“Storage for Essential Belongings”), the City cannot store potentially
contaminated items, as the Public Health Officer has advised that Leptospira can survive on contaminated
materials for weeks. Requiring the City to accept such items into storage would impose an undue burden,
given the substantial administrative effort that would be needed either to mitigate those risks or to
decontaminate the storage facility after the items were kept there. This is consistent with the City’s storage
policy that generally excludes the storage of items presenting a health or safety risk. The City has repeatedly
conducted direct public health outreach at the encampment and provided several rounds of notices informing
residents of its inability to handle or store belongings, and it has repeatedly communicated the same
information to you, to try to minimize negative impacts on those remaining at the encampment.
Re your sixth enumerated request (“ADA-Exempted Footprint”), the request for an indefinite or permanent
footprint on any public sidewalk, even provided a four-foot pedestrian clearance is maintained, cannot be
granted. Public sidewalks serve multiple legally protected purposes, including pedestrian travel (and
associated accessibility compliance obligations), emergency access, commercial activity conducted under
active City permits, and placement of utilities and public infrastructure. Sidewalk widths vary considerably,
and in many locations a four-foot clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once curb ramps,

Re your seventh enumerated request (“Language Access Accommodations”), The City responded to your

Re your eighth enumerated request (“Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement”), the City is unable to

Re your ninth enumerated request (“Visual Aid on Relocation Options”), the ADA requires public entities to

concerns, and legal requirements. Park rules prohibit overnight camping and unattended property exceeding
ten square feet without a permit. The Berkeley Municipal Code and Administrative Regulation 10.2 generally

In limited circumstances, individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not exceeding nine
square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained, facilities are not obstructed, and the location is not
designated as a no lodging area. Even in those circumstances, all individuals remain subject to enforcement
based on health and safety impacts, nuisance conditions, and compliance with applicable law. Because
enforcement determinations are location specific, condition dependent, and responsive to changing
circumstances, it is not possible for the City to identify locations where camping would be categorically free
from enforcement.

illustrate areas less likely to draw enforcement would require extensive staff time, site-specific analysis, and

Hi Mr. Gregory and Homeless Response Team,
I am writing on behalf of Hector Navarro, a disabled member of the Berkeley Homeless Union currently
residing in a pallet tiny home on 8th Street near UC Village, to formally submit a request for reasonable
accommodations.
Please find below the information required to initiate this request:
1. Disability Verification
Mr. Navarro lives with documented disabilities, including:
Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD)
Depression
Anxiety
High blood pressure
Hernia in the right side of his abdomen, which limits him to lifting no more than 10 pounds.
These conditions substantially limit one or more major life activities, including walking, standing, lifting,
carrying, sleeping, concentrating, regulating emotions and handling stress. His hernia causes chronic pain
and mobility limitations, while his mental health disabilities impair his ability to process stress and navigate
complex systems.
Mr. Navarro receives care from the Lifelong Street Medicine Team. We are in the process of obtaining
medical verification from his provider and will forward it to you as soon as it becomes available. Given the
urgency of the enforcement timeline, we ask that the City proceed with the interactive process based on
the disabilities self-reported in his signed accommodation request form (attached), pending receipt of
formal verification.

ER-808
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (173 of 289)

## ER-809

2. Nexus Between Disabilities and the City's Encampment Policies
The City's standard encampment management policies—as outlined in the February 17, 2026 Public
Notice—will, if applied to Mr. Navarro without modification, foreseeably aggravate his disabilities in the
following ways:
a) Short-notice relocation
His PTSD, anxiety and depression require predictability and stability. Sudden displacement triggers panic,
emotional dysregulation and physical stress responses that elevate his blood pressure and exacerbate his
mental health symptoms.
b) Physical demands of relocation
Due to his hernia, Mr. Navarro cannot lift more than 10 pounds. Requiring him to pack and move his
belongings without assistance would cause severe pain, potential injury and hernia complications. His high
blood pressure also makes physical exertion dangerous.
c) 9-square-foot limit on sidewalks
Compliance is physically impossible without abandoning his tiny home (his medical equipment and only
accessible shelter), essential belongings and his friend, who is his caregiver. The space is too small for
him and his caregiver to rest or store his necessities.
d) Threat of citation, arrest and property destruction
Penalizing Mr. Navarro for disability-related inability to comply—such as moving too slowly, being unable to
pack within time limits, or being unable to lift his belongings—would constitute discrimination under the
ADA. The stress of such encounters elevates his blood pressure that could lead to cardiac arrest and
triggers PTSD symptoms.
e) No property storage due to leptospirosis
Losing all of his belongings—including medications and items essential to daily survival—would be
harmful. For someone with high blood pressure and PTSD, losing familiar, essential items can trigger
acute psychological crises and dangerous blood pressure spikes.
f) Forced relocation without an accessible destination
Moving Mr. Navarro from one outdoor location to another does not achieve the City's stated goal of
"moving all people indoors." It merely destabilizes him, disrupts access to medical care, and exposes him
to repeated physical strain and stress—each time endangering his health.
g) Language barriers
Mr. Navarro is a Spanish speaker. The City has not provided any notices, instructions or services in
Spanish, leaving him unable to understand his rights, the enforcement timeline, or how to access available
resources. This language barrier:
Prevents him from meaningfully participating in the interactive process and City services, programs
and activities;
Exacerbates his anxiety and stress, as he cannot understand what is happening or what is expected
of him;
Compounds his disability-related limitations, making compliance impossible;
May constitute language discrimination under Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, which prohibits
discrimination based on national origin in federally funded programs.
3. Requested Accommodations
Based on Mr. Navarro's completed accommodation request form, his disabilities, and his language access
needs, we respectfully request the following accommodations:

## ER-810

1) Temporary Stay-in-Place Pending Accessible Housing
If no non-congregate shelter is immediately available, Mr. Navarro must be permitted to remain temporarily
in the 8th and Harrison area until an accessible room becomes available. Stability is medically necessary
to prevent the physical and psychological stress that elevates his blood pressure and triggers PTSD
symptoms.
2) A Safe Relocation Option
Mr. Navarro requires a clearly designated, safe location where he can relocate without fear of citation,
arrest, or property destruction. This location must:
Be accessible to someone with mental health disabilities
Provide access to basic sanitation, including trash pick up
Be within reasonable distance to food, social and medical services
3) Non-Congregate Shelter Placement (with Modified Visitor Policy)
A hotel room or other private, low-stimulation shelter unit with a modified visitor policy that allows him to
maintain family and community connections, including his caregiver—critical for his mental health.
Congregate settings are medically inappropriate as they exacerbate anxiety and PTSD. The visitor policy
modification is necessary to prevent isolation and depression.
4) Physical Assistance with Relocation
Due to his 10-pound lifting restriction, Mr. Navarro requires City staff assistance with lifting and transporting
his essential belongings to any new location. This assistance must be scheduled in advance.
5) Storage for Essential Belongings
We acknowledge the City's concern regarding leptospirosis. However, we request that the City identify a
safe storage option for Mr. Navarro's essential items—particularly his mobility devices, medications and
items necessary for daily survival. If storage is impossible, we request advance notice and assistance to
transport his belongings to another safe location.
6) ADA-Exempted Footprint
A permanent accommodation allowing Mr. Navarro to maintain a footprint larger than the standard 9-
square-foot limit—sufficient to accommodate himself, his caregiver and his essential belongings—on any
public sidewalk where:
He is not blocking pedestrian access, meaning a clear path remains for others to pass safely; and
If located on a narrower sidewalk, the opposite side of the street remains clear for alternative
pedestrian access.
This modification recognizes that for Mr. Navarro, his pallet tiny home functions as medical equipment and
his only accessible shelter.
7) Language Access Accommodations
Mr. Navarro is a Spanish speaker and requires all communications to be provided in Spanish. We request:
All written notices (including enforcement notices, accommodation determinations, and instructional
materials) be provided in Spanish;
A Spanish-speaking point of contact in the City designated for all interactions;
Interpreters present at all meetings, including the interactive process meeting;
Spanish-language visual aids showing relocation options and permissible locations;
The Berkeley Homeless Union is available to assist with interpretation if the City lacks Spanish-speaking
staff, but this does not relieve the City of its obligation to provide meaningful access. Failure to provide
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 152 of 165
ER-810
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (175 of 289)

## ER-811

language access may violate Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 and undermines the ADA interactive
process.
8) Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement (in Spanish)
Due to his PTSD, anxiety, and language barriers, Mr. Navarro requires advance written notice in Spanish
of any enforcement, cleaning, or relocation activity affecting his location—with at least 30 days' advance
notice—to allow him time to:
Process the information without acute stress
Arrange for support during the transition
Prepare his belongings at a pace that does not endanger his health
Coordinate with Spanish-speaking advocates
9) Visual Aid on Relocation Options (in Spanish)
A simple, visual map, diagram, list, or document in Spanish showing where he may legally relocate within
the city, including areas where tents and structures are permitted. This reduces uncertainty and stress.
10) Point of Contact in Spanish
A single, consistent point of contact in the City who speaks Spanish to help Mr. Navarro navigate the
relocation process. Frequent changes in staff or communicating through untranslated English will cause
confusion, increase anxiety and trigger stress-related health complications.
4. Request for Interactive Process Meeting
We respectfully request an in-person meeting with the ADA Coordinator, HRT, Mr. Navarro, a Union
representative, his medical provider (Lifelong Street Medicine Team), and a qualified Spanish interpreter to
discuss these accommodations in detail and ensure a thorough, individualized interactive process. All
meeting materials and discussions must be interpreted in Spanish.
5. Immediate Next Steps Requested
To fulfill the ADA's requirements, avoid foreseeable harm to Mr. Navarro, and ensure compliance with Title
VI language access obligations, we ask the City to:
Pause any enforcement activity toward Mr. Navarro pending full review of this request and
completion of the interactive process;
Confirm receipt of this request within 48 hours;
Provide available dates for a meeting with Spanish interpretation;
Designate a Spanish-speaking point of contact immediately;
Provide a substantive response in Spanish to each of the requested accommodations listed above;
Suggest alternatives in Spanish if any of the requested accommodations are deemed not feasible, so
that we can continue the interactive process in good faith.
We ask that the City not delay the interactive process while awaiting medical documentation, given the
imminent enforcement deadline.
Mr. Navarro is ready and willing to engage in good faith. The Berkeley Homeless Union stands ready to
assist with Spanish interpretation if needed, but the City must ensure that all communications are
accessible to Mr. Navarro in his primary language.
We look forward to working collaboratively to ensure his disability-related needs are met and his rights
under the ADA and Title VI are protected.
Thank you,

## ER-813

From: Gregory, Thomas
To: Berkeley Homeless Union
Cc: clear14u@gmail.com; Vance-Dozier, Okeya; Radu, Peter
Subject: Re: REQUEST: ADA Accommodation for Jonny Ruel Holder
Date: Monday, March 09, 2026 3:24:37 PM
Attachments: Harrison abatement notice + vehicles - FOURTH POSTING.pdf
Hi Yesica,
Thank you for reaching out on behalf of Mr. Holder.
You have submitted substantially similar requests on behalf of numerous individuals
over the past several months, and you have previously been advised that such requests
would constitute a fundamental alteration of the City’s operations, impose an undue
burden, or, in the case of shelter, do not constitute a form of reasonable
accommodation under the ADA, regardless of any particular individual’s disabilities.
Under Title II of the ADA, the City may deny requests based on fundamental alteration or
undue burden, because of the impact that a particular request would have on the City
and its programs or services. As I have previously informed you, those impacts to the
City are the same, regardless of the individual’s disability or the nexus for their request.
As such, even if there were a nexus between Mr. Holder requests and his required
relocation, the following determinations would still apply.
The determinations below, including alternatives where feasible, were made by me in
consultation with the City Manager’s Office and relevant City departments and staff.
Notice and Alleged Delay
The encampment at 6th and Harrison has been on notice since January 2025. Multiple
subsequent notices have been posted. In addition, City staff have repeatedly visited the
encampment, particularly after the discovery of leptospirosis-related public health
concerns, to encourage individuals to relocate away from the area for their own health
and safety.
Your statement that Mr. Holder approached HRT on February 5, 2026, prior to the most
recent abatement notice, further confirms that this is not a short-notice situation.
With respect to the assertion of “delay in proper guidance,” a reasonable
accommodation must relate to the application of an existing policy, practice, or
enforcement action. The City cannot modify or suspend a policy that has not yet been
applied. Prior to the issuance of a posted notice or enforcement directive, there is no
specific policy application to modify. Moreover, Mr. Holder has contacted me directly
several times since at least 2025 regarding other accommodation matters and is familiar
with how to submit requests. Under these circumstances, there was no denial of access
to the accommodation process.
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 155 of 165
ER-813
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (178 of 289)

## ER-814

You also state that Mr. Holder did not receive a copy of the public notice. The City has
attempted to post and maintain individual notices on at least three occasions. However,
staff have observed that individual notices have repeatedly been removed or destroyed
by a Berkeley Homeless Union member. This limits the City’s ability to ensure
continuous physical posting. When I attempted to speak directly with Mr. Holder earlier
this week regarding his reasonable accommodation needs, he did not indicate that he
had not received notice or request a copy. If he would like a copy delivered personally or
sent electronically, please let me know and I will provide one promptly. A copy of the
notice is attached here as well.
Requested Accommodations
a) Interim Housing Placement (hotel or private non-congregate unit)
The ADA requires reasonable modifications to policies where necessary to avoid
disability-based discrimination. The City maintains that the provisions of shelter or
housing itself is not a form of reasonable accommodation under the ADA. However,
once a City-sponsored shelter placement becomes available, the City is obligated to
consider reasonable accommodations related to the shelter environment itself, to
ensure equal access to that program.
When shelter placement is offered to Mr. Holder, he may request a disability-related
modification in that context. At present, the ADA does not require the City to provide a
specific housing outcome, such as a privately billed hotel room in another jurisdiction,
or to bypass established shelter eligibility and prioritization systems absent a showing
that those systems deny equal access because of disability.
Shelter options are available that would accept his service animal. I understand from
prior communications that Mr. Holder has declined both congregate and noncongregate shelter options within the City and has expressed interest only in permanent
supportive housing and a private hotel room in San Francisco in the interim. I continue to
encourage him to engage with HRT to explore available options and to request
modifications in that context if needed.
b) Designated Enforcement-Free Relocation Area
There are no enforcement-free areas within the City. The City reiterates that it does not
maintain sanctioned encampment areas and that its encampment regulations are
reflected in municipal code provisions, administrative regulations, and City Council
resolutions that apply citywide and are subject to change. The City’s encampmentrelated requirements are established through a combination of municipal code
provisions, administrative regulations, and City Council resolutions, all of which may be
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 156 of 165
ER-814
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (179 of 289)

## ER-815

updated in response to public health conditions, safety concerns, and legal
requirements. 
For example:
Park rules prohibit overnight camping and unattended property exceeding ten
square feet without a permit.
The Berkeley Municipal Code and Administrative Regulation 10.2 generally prohibit
structures exceeding nine square feet on sidewalks.
Those provisions also prohibit lodging or storage of property on medians and
within City building curtilage.
In limited circumstances, individuals may remain on certain sidewalks with property not
exceeding nine square feet, provided pedestrian access is maintained, facilities are not
obstructed, and the location is not designated as a no-lodging area. Even in those
circumstances, individuals remain subject to enforcement based on health and safety
impacts, nuisance conditions, and compliance with applicable law.
Because enforcement determinations are location-specific, condition-dependent, and
responsive to changing circumstances, including sanitation, obstruction, emergency
access, and public health factors, it is not possible for the City to identify locations
where camping would be categorically free from enforcement.
Creating a designated enforcement-free zone for one individual would require
suspending generally applicable municipal code provisions and administrative
regulations in a defined geographic area. That would constitute a fundamental alteration
of the City’s regulatory framework and is not required by the ADA. As an alternative, I
have previously provided a summary of applicable rules.
c) 30 Days’ Advance Written Notice of Any Enforcement
The request for a guaranteed 30-day advance written notice before any future
enforcement action seeks a categorical modification of the City’s enforcement
timelines.
The City already provides notice periods that exceed minimum constitutional and
statutory requirements. These extended notice periods are intended, in part, to account
for the fact that individuals with disabilities may require additional time to review,
process, and comply.
Imposing a mandatory 30-day delay in all circumstances would:
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 157 of 165
ER-815
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (180 of 289)

## ER-816

1. Create an Undue Administrative Burden – The City must respond to evolving
conditions, including blocked rights of way, fire hazards, infrastructure damage,
and sanitation failures. A fixed 30-day delay would require the City to suspend
standard enforcement timelines and track individualized notice guarantees across
changing conditions, which would not be operationally feasible on a citywide
basis.
2. Fundamentally Alter Enforcement Authority – The City’s health and safety
enforcement framework depends on the ability to respond proportionally and
promptly to conditions on the ground. A guaranteed delay, regardless of
circumstances, would substantially alter how the City carries out its legal
obligations.
3. Undermine Public Health Response – In light of the leptospirosis-related
conditions at this location, the City must retain the ability to act quickly to mitigate
health risks and the spread of the disease. The ADA does not require modifications
that would create or perpetuate a direct threat to the health or safety of others. A
fixed 30-day delay, especially where urgent conditions exist, would undermine that
obligation.
For these reasons, this request cannot be granted.
d) Visual Map of Permissible Locations
Because there are no sanctioned encampments or enforcement-free zones, and
because enforcement decisions depend on specific, evolving conditions rather than
fixed geographic boundaries, the City cannot provide a map identifying areas where
tents are categorically permitted.
As an alternative, I have provided summaries of applicable rules.
e) Recognition of Tent as Medical Equipment
The City does not intend to seize or destroy Mr. Holder’s tent. The purpose of advance
notice is to allow voluntary relocation before any abatement activity occurs.
The ADA does not require the City to designate a tent located on public property as
medical equipment exempt from generally applicable health and safety laws. However,
consistent with established practice, the City provides notice specifically to allow
individuals to move with their belongings intact.
Mr. Holder has been aware of the abatement process since at least February 5 and has
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 158 of 165
ER-816
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (181 of 289)

## ER-817

been repeatedly encouraged to engage with HRT regarding relocation and shelter
options.
f) Physical Assistance with Relocation
You state that Mr. Holder requires assistance to avoid physical strain. However, no
physical mobility impairment or lifting limitation has been identified, and Mr. Holder has
not previously indicated such limitations in our discussions.
A reasonable accommodation requires a clear nexus between the disability and the
requested modification. As such, that nexus has not been established.
g) Temporary Stay-in-Place
The City cannot grant a temporary pause in enforcement at this location. The
encampment conditions contributing to the leptospirosis-related public health
concerns must be addressed.
Allowing continued occupation at this site would delay mitigation of sanitation and
hinder the ability of the city to address the extensive rodent population that is the
primary vector for leptospirosis. Where a requested modification would prevent the City
from addressing conditions that pose a direct threat to health and safety, the ADA does
not require that modification.
Given the length of prior notice and outreach, there is no ADA-based basis for extending
occupancy at this location in a manner that would interfere with public health response.
h) ADA-Exempted 12’×12’ Footprint on Public Sidewalks
The request for a permanent 140-square-foot footprint on any public sidewalk, provided
a four-foot pedestrian path is maintained, cannot be granted.
Public sidewalks serve multiple legally protected functions:
Pedestrian travel, including accessibility compliance obligations;
Emergency and fire access;
Commercial activity conducted under active and ongoing City permits (including
outdoor dining, vending, retail displays, and other authorized uses);
Placement of utilities, street furniture, and other public infrastructure.
Sidewalk width varies considerably throughout the City. In many locations, maintaining
only a four-foot clearance would not satisfy accessibility requirements once curb
ramps, obstructions, turning radii, and permitted commercial installations are
Case 3:25-cv-01414-EMC Document 261-1 Filed 03/09/26 Page 159 of 165
ER-817
Case: 26-2755, 07/27/2026, DktEntry: 30.5, (182 of 289)

## ER-818

accounted for.
Numerous sidewalks are subject to active permits authorizing businesses to place
tables, merchandise, or equipment in the public right of way. Granting a blanket
exemption for a 12’×12’ structure would conflict with these permitted commercial uses
and disrupt established allocation of limited sidewalk space.
For these reasons, this request would fundamentally alter the City’s management of the
public right of way and cannot be approved.
i) Recognition of Dog as Medical Equipment
Mr. Holder currently has his dog with him. The City does not intend to separate him from
a service animal. Service animals are permitted consistent with applicable law. Because
no policy is being applied that would exclude his dog, no modification is required at this
time.
Final Determination
This letter constitutes my final determination regarding the requested accommodations,
and this determination is based upon the reasons described above, and due to the fact
that the request are not supported by sufficient information showing the disabilityrelated need for the requested accommodation, and because the requested
accommodation would impede actions necessary to protect public health. If
Mr. Holder wishes to file a grievance regarding this decision, he may do so using the
City’s ADA grievance procedure available here: https://berkeleyca.gov/yourgovernment/our-work/disability-compliance-program.
I remain committed to engaging respectfully and in good faith and encourage
Mr. Holder to work with HRT to explore available shelter and housing navigation options.
Best,
Thomas Gregory / ADA Program Coordinator
